4
The Poisson equation. Simplicial meshes and spatial discretization error

In this section, we start with the simplest partial differential equation for diffusion processes: the Poisson equation. We first recall the definition of a weak solution based on the Sobolev space
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
of Section
3.2.1
and show that the associated flux lies in the Sobolev space
\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
of Section
3.2.2
. This is very instrumental and will be repeated in all generalizations of Sections
5
–
7
below. We then define the energy error and explore its structure for a completely generic approximate solution. We subsequently consider a basic cell-centered finite volume discretization incarnating the principles of Section
3.7
. We only employ in this section a simplicial mesh as per Section
3.3
and define the approximate solution by lifting the face fluxes into a Raviart–Thomas piecewise polynomial space of Section
3.5.3
. Next, we introduce and discuss two central notions of this work: the flux and potential reconstructions. These in particular lead to a posteriori error estimates fulfilling all the building principles of Section
2
. Illustrative numerical experiments close this section.

4.1
The Poisson equation

In this section, for the domain
\Omega
as specified in Section
3.1.1
, we consider the problem of finding
p:\Omega\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
such that

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\nabla{\cdot}(\nabla p)

\displaystyle=f\qquad\mbox{ in }\,\Omega,

(4.1a)

\displaystyle\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=0\qquad\mbox{ on }\,\partial\Omega.

(4.1b)

Here
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
is a source term.

4.2
Weak solution, potential, and flux

The Sobolev space
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
from Definition
3.4
gives a proper mathematical setting to define a unique solution
p
of (
4.1
). Indeed, there is one and only one
p\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
such that

(\nabla p,\nabla v)=(f,v)\qquad\forall v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega);

(4.2)

note that this follows trivially from the Riesz representation theorem, since
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
is a Hilbert space for the scalar product given by
(\nabla p,\nabla v)
and since
(f,v)
is bounded linear form over
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
. We call (
4.2
) the weak formulation of (
4.1
). From the weak solution
p
, we can define the dual variable

\bm{u}:=-\nabla p.

(4.3)

In relation to the applications below,
p
may be a pressure and
\bm{u}
a Darcy velocity, but we rather generically refer to
p
as
potential
and to
\bm{u}
as
flux
.

4.3
Properties of the exact potential and flux

Using Definitions
3.4
and
3.7
of the spaces
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
and
\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
, we have the following important result, detailing what we have discovered in (
2.5
):

Proposition 4.1

(Properties of the weak solution (
4.2
))
.

Let

p
be the solution of (
4.2
). Let
\bm{u}
be given by (
4.3
). Then

p\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega),\quad\bm{u}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega),\quad\nabla{\cdot}\bm{u}=f.

(4.4)

Proof.

The weak solution
p
belongs to
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
by definition. In order
to verify that
\bm{u}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
and
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{u}=f
, we need to check the three conditions
of Definition
3.6
. Condition
1
is obvious,
as
p\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
and thus
-\nabla p=\bm{u}
is square-integrable in view of Definition
3.1
, condition
2a
.
For the function
w
of condition
2a
in Definition
3.6
, choose
w:=f

and note that
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
by assumption. Then
condition
2b
follows immediately
from (
4.2
) and the fact that
{\mathcal{D}}(\Omega)\subset H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
. ∎

Remark 4.2

(Mathematical properties of the weak solution)
.

It is remarkable that the flux
\bm{u}
prescribed from the potential
p
by (
4.3
) belongs to
\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
and has a divergence in equilibrium with the load
f
, since this is not explicitly stipulated in the weak formulation (
4.2
). Congruently, it immediately follows (cf. Proposition
4.4
below) that the three properties (
4.4
) together with the constitutive relation (
4.3
) actually define the weak solution
p
of (
4.2
) in a unique way. This mathematically describes the intrinsic properties of the exact potential
p
and flux
\bm{u}
:
p
has to be trace continuous and
\bm{u}
has to be related by the constitutive relation (
4.3
) to it, normal-trace continuous, and in equilibrium with the load.

Remark 4.3

(Physical properties of the weak solution)
.

Scalar-valued physical variables such as the potential (pressure)
p
are naturally trace continuous, whereas vector-valued physical variables such as the flux (Darcy velocity)
\bm{u}=-\nabla p
are naturally normal-trace continuous, cf. Figures
1
and
2
. Moreover,
\bm{u}
has to be in equilibrium with the load (locally mass conservative) which amounts to

\nabla{\cdot}\bm{u}=f
. This restates that the three properties (
4.4
) together with the constitutive relation (
4.3
) characterize the exact solution.

4.4
Approximate solution and spatial discretization error

Let
\bm{u}_{h}\in\bm{L}^{2}(\Omega)
be an arbitrary function that we think of a (numerical) approximation of the exact flux
\bm{u}
from (
4.2
)–(
4.3
). We will give a specific example for the cell-centered finite volume discretizations on a simplicial mesh in Section
4.8
below, but we want to proceed abstractly for the moment, since there is no link to a mesh or to the discrete world of piecewise polynomials at this stage. We will call the
\bm{L}^{2}(\Omega)
-distance of
\bm{u}_{h}
to
\bm{u}
,

\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}\|,

(4.5)

the
spatial discretization error
.

4.5
Prager–Synge equality

We now state the following important result dating back to Prager and Synge
[
216
]
:

Proposition 4.4

(Prager–Synge equality)
.

Let
p\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
be the weak solution of (
4.2
) and let
\zeta_{h}\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
and
\bm{\sigma}_{h}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
with
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{\sigma}_{h}=f
be arbitrary. Then

\|\nabla(p-\zeta_{h})\|^{2}+\|\nabla p+\bm{\sigma}_{h}\|^{2}=\|\nabla\zeta_{h}+\bm{\sigma}_{h}\|^{2}.

(4.6)

Proof.

Adding and subtracting
\nabla p
, we develop

\displaystyle\|\nabla\zeta_{h}+\bm{\sigma}_{h}\|^{2}{}

\displaystyle=\|\nabla(\zeta_{h}-p)+\nabla p+\bm{\sigma}_{h}\|^{2}

\displaystyle{}=\|\nabla(\zeta_{h}-p)\|^{2}+\|\nabla p+\bm{\sigma}_{h}\|^{2}+2(\nabla(\zeta_{h}-p),\nabla p+\bm{\sigma}_{h}).

Note from Proposition
4.1
that
\nabla p\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
with
\nabla{\cdot}(\nabla p)=-f
. Thus
(\nabla p+\bm{\sigma}_{h})\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
and in
particular
\nabla{\cdot}(\nabla p+\bm{\sigma}_{h})=0
. Thus, using that
\zeta_{h}-p\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
, the Green theorem (
3.3
) gives

(\underbrace{\nabla p+\bm{\sigma}_{h}}_{\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)},\nabla(\underbrace{\zeta_{h}-p}_{\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq_Green_Hdv}}}{{=}}-(\underbrace{\nabla{\cdot}(\nabla p+\bm{\sigma}_{h})}_{=0},\zeta_{h}-p)=0,

whence the assertion follows. ∎

Remark 4.5

(Prager–Synge equality (
4.6
))
.

We stress that (
4.6
) is an equality, where on the right-hand side, there are, in practice, two known, discrete objects from the piecewise polynomial subspaces of
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
and
\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
, whereas the left-hand side are their spatial discretization errors with respect to the weak solution of (
4.2
). In other words, if we want to trace a simultaneous potential and flux error, then we can compute it, there is no (a posteriori) estimate.

From (
4.6
), we immediately observe (cf. Theorem 6.1 and Corollary 6.6 in
[
250
]
and the references therein):

Corollary 4.6

(Prager–Synge (in)equality)
.

Let
p\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
be the solution of (
4.2
), let
\bm{u}=-\nabla p
from (
4.3
), and let
\bm{u}_{h}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
with
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{u}_{h}=f
be arbitrary. Then

\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}\|=\min_{v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)}\|\bm{u}_{h}+\nabla v\|{,}

(4.7)

so that in particular

\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}\|\leq\|\bm{u}_{h}+\nabla\zeta_{h}\|

(4.8)

for an arbitrary
\zeta_{h}\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
.

Proof.

The second assertion (
4.8
) follows from (
4.6
) upon taking
\bm{\sigma}_{h}=\bm{u}_{h}
and noticing that

\|\nabla p+\bm{u}_{h}\|^{2}=\|\nabla\zeta_{h}+\bm{u}_{h}\|^{2}-\|\nabla(p-\zeta_{h})\|^{2}\leq\|\nabla\zeta_{h}+\bm{u}_{h}\|^{2}

for any
\zeta_{h}\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
, using
\bm{u}=-\nabla p
from (
4.3
). This immediately gives (
4.7
) with infimum in place of minimum and the
\leq
sign in place of the equality. Since we can take
\zeta_{h}=p
and since
\bm{u}=-\nabla p
, the minimum in (
4.7
) is actually attained and the right-hand side equals the left-hand side. ∎

Remark 4.7

(Prager–Synge equality (
4.7
))
.

According to (
4.3
) and (
4.4
), to be equal to the exact flux
-\nabla p
, any
\bm{u}_{h}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
with
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{u}_{h}=f
only misses being a minus weak gradient of some function from
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
. This is the meaning of the characterization (
4.7
). Notice that (
4.7
) expresses the distance of
\bm{u}_{h}
to
\nabla H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
.

Remark 4.8

(Prager–Synge inequality (
4.8
))
.

Having
\bm{u}_{h}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
with
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{u}_{h}=f
at disposal, which we will see in Section
4.8
below is immediate in finite volume methods for the Poisson equation (
4.1
), when
f
is piecewise constant, the Prager–Synge inequality (
4.8
) can be readily used to produce a guaranteed error upper bound also satisfying the other requirements formulated in Section
2.5
.
This will still be the situation for the singlephase steady linear Darcy flow in Section
5
below, but when we want to take into account iterative algebraic solvers in Section
6
and component fluxes together with nonlinear zero-order terms in Section
7
for nonlinear compositional Darcy flows, it may not be feasible to locally construct
\bm{u}_{h}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
with
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{u}_{h}=f
as an approximate solution. There, we will in general only have
\bm{u}_{h}\in\bm{L}^{2}(\Omega)
. In this case, we will reconstruct
\bm{\sigma}_{h}^{k,i}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
with
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{\sigma}_{h}^{k,i}=f
or
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{\sigma}_{h}^{k,i}=f+\rho_{h}^{k,i}
with
\rho_{h}^{k,i}
a remainder made as small as necessary.

4.6
Equivalence of the spatial discretization error to the flux nonconformity (dual norm of the residual) plus potential nonconformity

Following
[
213
, Theorem 3.1]
,
[
125
, Theorem 3.3]
, and the references therein, we now extend Corollary
4.6
to the general case
\bm{u}_{h}\in\bm{L}^{2}(\Omega)
.

Theorem 4.9

(Error characterization for
\bm{u}_{h}\in\bm{L}^{2}(\Omega)
)
.

Let
p\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
be the weak solution of (
4.2
), let
\bm{u}=-\nabla p
from (
4.3
), and let
\bm{u}_{h}\in\bm{L}^{2}(\Omega)
be arbitrary. Then

\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}\|^{2}=\underbrace{\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{v}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)\\
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{v}=f\end{subarray}}\|\bm{u}_{h}-\bm{v}\|^{2}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\max_{\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega);\,\|\nabla\varphi\|=1}\{(f,\varphi)+(\bm{u}_{h},\nabla\varphi)\}^{2},\\
\text{dual norm of the residual of }\bm{u}_{h}\end{subarray}}+\underbrace{\min_{v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)}\|\bm{u}_{h}+\nabla v\|^{2}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{nonconformity}\\
(\text{distance of }\bm{u}_{h}\text{ to }\nabla H^{1}_{0}(\Omega))\end{subarray}}.

(4.9)

Remark 4.10

(Error characterization (
4.9
))
.

Relation (
4.9
) extends (
4.7
) from
\bm{u}_{h}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
with
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{u}_{h}=f
to any
\bm{u}_{h}\in\bm{L}^{2}(\Omega)
. The supplementary arising term is naturally the distance of
\bm{u}_{h}
to
\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
under the constraint of the divergence being equal to
f
.

Remark 4.11

(Dual norm of the residual)
.

The first term in (
4.9
) has an equivalent expression investigating “how much, for a test function
\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
,
\bm{u}_{h}
misses to fulfill the weak formulation (
4.2
) when put in place of
-\nabla p
”, which is related to the residual of (
4.2
) for
\varphi
. The overall maximum over all
\varphi
from
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
with
\|\nabla\varphi\|=1
is then the dual norm of the residual.

Remark 4.12

(Potential nonconformity)
.

The second term in (
4.9
) expresses the distance of
\bm{u}_{h}
to
\nabla H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
, cf. Remark
4.7
.

Proof of Theorem
4.9
.

Let us define a function
\zeta\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)

by

(\nabla\zeta,\nabla v)=-(\bm{u}_{h},\nabla v)\qquad\forall v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega).

(4.10)

There exists one and only one
\zeta
by the Riesz representation theorem; indeed, recall from Section
3.2.1
that the left-hand side of (
4.10
) is a scalar product on
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
and notice that from
|-(\bm{u}_{h},\nabla v)|\leq\|\bm{u}_{h}\|\|\nabla v\|
, the right-hand side of (
4.10
) is a bounded linear form on
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
(
\bm{u}_{h}
is here a fixed datum). The function
\zeta
can be seen as the orthogonal projection of the approximate solution
\bm{u}_{h}
onto
\nabla H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
. With the aid of
\zeta
, we can thus write the Pythagorean equality

\|\nabla p+\bm{u}_{h}\|^{2}=\|\nabla(p-\zeta)\|^{2}+\|\nabla\zeta+\bm{u}_{h}\|^{2}.

(4.11)

Indeed,

\|\nabla p+\bm{u}_{h}\|^{2}=\|\nabla p-\nabla\zeta+\nabla\zeta+\bm{u}_{h}\|^{2}=\|\nabla(p-\zeta)\|^{2}+\|\nabla\zeta+\bm{u}_{h}\|^{2}+2(\nabla(p-\zeta),\nabla\zeta+\bm{u}_{h}),

and the last term in the above expression vanishes in view of the orthogonality (
4.10
), since
p-\zeta
can be taken as a test function
v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
in (
4.10
). We continue in three steps.

1) Since
\zeta
can be seen as a projection of
\bm{u}_{h}
,

\|\nabla\zeta+\bm{u}_{h}\|^{2}=\min_{v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)}\|\nabla v+\bm{u}_{h}\|^{2}.

(4.12)

Indeed, from (
4.11
) used for an arbitrary function
v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
in place of
p\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
, we see

\|\nabla v+\bm{u}_{h}\|^{2}=\|\nabla(v-\zeta)\|^{2}+\|\nabla\zeta+\bm{u}_{h}\|^{2}.

Therefrom, we get

\|\nabla\zeta+\bm{u}_{h}\|^{2}=\|\nabla v+\bm{u}_{h}\|^{2}-\|\nabla(v-\zeta)\|^{2}\leq\|\nabla v+\bm{u}_{h}\|^{2}\qquad\forall v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega){,}

and there is an equality for
v=\zeta
.
This shows that the second term in (
4.11
) indeed takes the form needed in (
4.9
).

2) For the first term in (
4.11
), we first use that
p-\zeta\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
. Thus, the dual norm characterization and (
4.10
) give

\|\nabla(p-\zeta)\|=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)\\
\|\nabla\varphi\|=1\end{subarray}}(\nabla(p-\zeta),\nabla\varphi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq_s}}}{{=}}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)\\
\|\nabla\varphi\|=1\end{subarray}}(\nabla p+\bm{u}_{h},\nabla\varphi).

(4.13)

Let now
\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
with
\|\nabla\varphi\|=1
be fixed. Using the
characterization (
4.2
) of the weak solution, we have

(\nabla p+\bm{u}_{h},\nabla\varphi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq_Pois_WF}}}{{=}}(f,\varphi)+(\bm{u}_{h},\nabla\varphi).

(4.14)

Thus, we have (
4.9
) with the first term in the form expressed in the underbrace.

3) We are left to show the equivalence

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{v}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)\\
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{v}=f\end{subarray}}\|\bm{u}_{h}-\bm{v}\|=\max_{\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega);\,\|\nabla\varphi\|=1}\{(f,\varphi)+(\bm{u}_{h},\nabla\varphi)\}.

(4.15)

3a) First, to motivate, we show that the right-hand side of (
4.15
) is bounded by the left-hand side one. Let
\bm{v}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
such that
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{v}=f
be arbitrary. Then the Green theorem (
3.3
) gives

(f,\varphi)+(\bm{u}_{h},\nabla\varphi)=(\nabla{\cdot}\bm{v},\varphi)+(\bm{u}_{h},\nabla\varphi)=(\bm{u}_{h}-\bm{v},\nabla\varphi).

Consequently, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

\max_{\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega);\,\|\nabla\varphi\|=1}\{(f,\varphi)+(\bm{u}_{h},\nabla\varphi)\}\leq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{v}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)\\
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{v}=f\end{subarray}}\|\bm{u}_{h}-\bm{v}\|.

(4.16)

3b) We now show the equality (
4.15
). The argument of the constrained minimization in (
4.15
) is

\bm{\sigma}:=\arg\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{v}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)\\
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{v}=f\end{subarray}}\|\bm{u}_{h}-\bm{v}\|

and is characterized by the Euler–Lagrange conditions as a function
\bm{\sigma}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
with
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{\sigma}=f
such that

(\bm{\sigma},\bm{v})=(\bm{u}_{h},\bm{v})\qquad\forall\bm{v}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)\text{ with }\nabla{\cdot}\bm{v}=0.

Imposing the constraint
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{\sigma}=f
equivalently with test functions
q\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and introducing the Lagrange multiplier
r\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, using that
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)=L^{2}(\Omega)
, the above problem is equivalent to finding
\bm{\sigma}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
and
r\in L^{2}(\Omega)
such that

\displaystyle(\bm{\sigma},\bm{v})-(r,\nabla{\cdot}\bm{v})

\displaystyle=(\bm{u}_{h},\bm{v})\qquad

\displaystyle\forall\bm{v}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega),

(4.17a)

\displaystyle(\nabla{\cdot}\bm{\sigma},q)

\displaystyle=(f,q)

\displaystyle\forall q\in L^{2}(\Omega).

(4.17b)

Now, (
4.17a
) implies by Definition
3.1
of the weak partial derivative that
r\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
with
\nabla r=-(\bm{\sigma}-\bm{u}_{h})
. Consequently, by the Green theorem (
3.3
),

\begin{split}\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{v}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)\\
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{v}=f\end{subarray}}\|\bm{u}_{h}-\bm{v}\|&=\|\bm{u}_{h}-\bm{\sigma}\|=\|\nabla r\|=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)\\
\|\nabla\varphi\|=1\end{subarray}}(\nabla r,\nabla\varphi)\\
&=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)\\
\|\nabla\varphi\|=1\end{subarray}}(-\bm{\sigma}+\bm{u}_{h},\nabla\varphi)=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)\\
\|\nabla\varphi\|=1\end{subarray}}\{(f,\varphi)+(\bm{u}_{h},\nabla\varphi)\},\end{split}

(4.18)

which is (
4.15
). ∎

4.7
Cell-centered finite volume discretizations on a simplicial mesh

Consider now a simplicial mesh
\mathcal{T}_{h}
of the domain
\Omega
as defined in Section
3.3
. We additionally assume that 1) there exists a point
\bm{x}_{K}
associated with each simplex
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
; 2) there exists a point
\bm{x}_{{K},{\sigma}}
associated with each face
{\sigma}
lying in the boundary
\partial\Omega
,
{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}\cap\mathcal{F}_{h}^{\mathrm{ext}}
,
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
, lying in the interior of the face
{\sigma}
; 3) all the points
\bm{x}_{K}
and
\bm{x}_{{K},{\sigma}}
are distinct; 4) the straight line connecting
\bm{x}_{K}
and
\bm{x}_{L}
for two neighboring elements
{K}
and
{L}
is orthogonal to their common face
{\sigma}_{{K},{L}}=\partial{K}\cap\partial{L}\in\mathcal{F}_{h}^{\mathrm{int}}
; 5) the straight line connecting
\bm{x}_{K}
and
\bm{x}_{{K},{\sigma}}
is orthogonal to
{\sigma}
for
{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}\cap\mathcal{F}_{h}^{\mathrm{ext}}
. The points
\bm{x}_{K}
typically, but not necessarily, lie in the interior of the element
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
. In particular, Delaunay meshes satisfy this requirement of “admissibility” in the sense of
[
126
, Definition 9.1]
. An illustration is provided in Figure
10
.

Figure 10:
Admissible triangular mesh
\mathcal{T}_{h}
and notation for the cell-centered finite volume scheme

The cell-centered finite volume scheme for the Poisson equation (
4.1
) on the simplicial mesh
\mathcal{T}_{h}
reads: find the real values
p_{K}
,
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
, the approximations to the mean values of
p
in the mesh elements
{K}
, such that

\boxed{\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}}U_{{K},{\sigma}}=(f,1)_{K}\qquad\forall{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h},}

(4.19a)

where
U_{{K},{\sigma}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
for each face
{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}
approximates the normal (out)flux
\langle\bm{u}{\cdot}\bm{n}_{K},1\rangle_{\sigma}
from
{K}
over the face
{\sigma}
(recall the notation (
3.12
)) by

\displaystyle U_{{K},{\sigma}}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{|{\sigma}_{{K},{L}}|}{d_{{K},{L}}}(p_{L}-p_{K})\qquad

\displaystyle{\sigma}={\sigma}_{{K},{L}}\in\mathcal{F}_{h}^{\mathrm{int}},

(4.19b)

\displaystyle U_{{K},{\sigma}}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{|{\sigma}|}{d_{{K},{\sigma}}}(0-p_{K})

\displaystyle{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}\cap\mathcal{F}_{h}^{\mathrm{ext}}.

(4.19c)

Recall that
|{\sigma}|
is the
(d-1)
-dimensional measure of the face
{\sigma}
. Moreover,
d_{{K},{L}}=|\bm{x}_{K}-\bm{x}_{L}|
is the Euclidean distance of
the points
\bm{x}_{K}
and
\bm{x}_{L}
for an interior face
{\sigma}={\sigma}_{{K},{L}}\in\mathcal{F}_{h}^{\mathrm{int}}
, unless the positions of
\bm{x}_{K}
and
\bm{x}_{L}
are interchanged with respect to the positions of
{K}
and
{L}
, where we take
d_{{K},{L}}=-|\bm{x}_{K}-\bm{x}_{L}|
. Since we suppose
\bm{x}_{K}
and
\bm{x}_{L}
distinct,
d_{{K},{L}}\neq 0
. Similarly,
d_{{K},{\sigma}}=|\bm{x}_{K}-\bm{x}_{{K},{\sigma}}|
is the Euclidean distance of the points
\bm{x}_{K}
and
\bm{x}_{{K},{\sigma}}
for a boundary face
{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}\cap\mathcal{F}_{h}^{\mathrm{ext}}
, unless
\bm{x}_{K}
lies outside of
{K}
past
{\sigma}
, where we take
d_{{K},{\sigma}}=-|\bm{x}_{K}-\bm{x}_{{K},{\sigma}}|
. Figure
10
provides an illustration.

Remark 4.13

(Flux conservation and equilibrium in finite volumes on simplicial meshes)
.

From (
4.19b
), there holds

U_{{K},{\sigma}}=-U_{{L},{\sigma}}\qquad\forall{\sigma}={\sigma}_{{K},{L}}\in\mathcal{F}_{h}^{\mathrm{int}}.

(4.20)

This is the flux conservation in finite volumes: what flows from the element
{K}
out through the face
{\sigma}_{{K},{L}}
flows in the neighbor element
{L}
through the same face
{\sigma}_{{K},{L}}
, cf. Lemma
3.11
and definition (
3.15
). The requirement (
4.19a
) then stipulates the equilibrium of these face fluxes with the load
f
, mimicking
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{u}=f
from Proposition
4.1
.

4.8
Flux reconstruction by lifting the face normal fluxes

Figure 11:
Finite volume face fluxes
U_{{K},{\sigma}}
(dashed black arrows) and flux reconstruction
\bm{u}_{h}
of Definition
4.14
(blue arrows)

Following
[
127
]
, we now lift the normal fluxes
U_{{K},{\sigma}}
from (
4.19b
)–(
4.19c
) to form a vector-valued approximate flux field
\bm{u}_{h}
that is
\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
-conforming and in equilibrium with the load
f
. More precisely, we construct
\bm{u}_{h}
in the piecewise polynomial Raviart–Thomas space
\bm{\mathcal{R\hskip-1.00006ptT}}\hskip-2.5pt_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{h})\cap\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
from (
3.15
).

Recalling the definition (
3.10
) of the space
\bm{\mathcal{R\hskip-1.00006ptT}}\hskip-2.5pt_{0}({K})
for a simplex
{K}
and the notation from Figure
6
, we start with:

Definition 4.14

(Flux reconstruction)
.

Let the cell-centered finite volume discretization on a simplicial mesh be given by (
4.19
). Then, on each simplex
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
, define

\bm{u}_{h}|_{K}\in\bm{\mathcal{R\hskip-1.00006ptT}}\hskip-2.5pt_{0}({K}),\quad\langle\bm{u}_{h}{\cdot}\bm{n}_{K},1\rangle_{\sigma}=U_{{K},{\sigma}}\qquad\forall{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}},

(4.21)

or, equivalently, using the Raviart–Thomas basis functions
\bm{v}_{\sigma}
from (
3.13
),

(\bm{u}_{h}|_{K})(\bm{x}):=\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}}U_{{K},{\sigma}}{\bm{v}_{\sigma}(\bm{x})=\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}}U_{{K},{\sigma}}}\frac{1}{d|{K}|}(\bm{x}-{\bm{a}}_{{K},{\sigma}}),\qquad\bm{x}\in{K}.

(4.22)

An illustration of the flux reconstruction
\bm{u}_{h}
from the finite volume face fluxes
U_{{K},{\sigma}}
is provided in Figure
11
.
Crucially, we have:

Lemma 4.15

(Flux reconstruction)
.

Let
\bm{u}_{h}
be given by Definition
4.14
. Then

\bm{u}_{h}\in\bm{\mathcal{R\hskip-1.00006ptT}}\hskip-2.5pt_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{h})\cap\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega),\qquad{(}\nabla{\cdot}\bm{u}_{h}{)|_{K}}=\frac{(f,1)_{K}}{|{K}|}\qquad\forall{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}.

(4.23)

Proof.

From the finite volume conservation (
4.20
) and definition (
4.21
), we have the normal trace continuity needed in (
3.15
); this yields the first property in (
4.23
). As for the second property in (
4.23
), we write, using the Green theorem, definition (
4.21
), and the finite volume scheme (
4.19a
)

(\nabla{\cdot}\bm{u}_{h},1)_{K}=\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}}\langle\bm{u}_{h}{\cdot}\bm{n}_{K},1\rangle_{\sigma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq_fr_Pois}}}{{=}}\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}}U_{{K},{\sigma}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq_FV_scheme}}}{{=}}(f,1)_{K}.

The conclusion follows since
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{u}_{h}
is constant by virtue of (
3.11
). ∎

4.9
Potential reconstruction by elementwise postprocessing and averaging

Supposing for the moment that
f
is piecewise constant, so that
\bm{u}_{h}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
with
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{u}_{h}=f
from (
4.23
), we will be in position to use Corollary
4.6
to estimate the error between the finite volume flux reconstruction
\bm{u}_{h}
of Definition
4.14
and the exact flux
\bm{u}=-\nabla p
. Inequality (
4.8
) tells us that we will need a suitable scalar-valued object in the primal space
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
whose negative gradient is as close as possible to
\bm{u}_{h}
. Since the piecewise constant function given by the finite volume values
p_{K}
on each
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
cannot be used directly, as it does not lie in the space
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
, we proceed in two steps.

Following
[
247
, Section 4.1]
and
[
249
, Section 3.2]
, we first create a scalar-valued piecewise polynomial whose negative gradient equals
\bm{u}_{h}
and whose mean values are given by
p_{K}
on each mesh element
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
. Recalling the piecewise polynomial space
{\mathcal{P}}_{k}(\mathcal{T}_{h})
from (
3.8
), we define:

Definition 4.16

(Potential postprocessing)
.

Let
p_{K}
,
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
, be the finite volume approximate solution of (
4.19
). Let
\bm{u}_{h}
be given by Definition
4.14
. Define the potential postprocessing
\tilde{p}_{h}
as a piecewise quadratic polynomial on the simplicial mesh
\mathcal{T}_{h}
,
\tilde{p}_{h}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathcal{T}_{h})
, given by

-\nabla\tilde{p}_{h}|_{K}=\bm{u}_{h}|_{K},\qquad\frac{(\tilde{p}_{h},1)_{K}}{|{K}|}=p_{K}\qquad\forall{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}.

(4.24)

Figure 12:
Potential postprocessing
\tilde{p}_{h}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathcal{T}_{h})
(left) and potential reconstruction by averaging
\zeta_{h}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathcal{T}_{h})\cap H^{1}(\Omega)
(right)

It is easy to verify that any object from
\bm{\mathcal{R\hskip-1.00006ptT}}\hskip-2.5pt_{0}({K})
for a simplex
{K}
is a gradient of some polynomial which is at most second-order, since fields in
\bm{\mathcal{R\hskip-1.00006ptT}}\hskip-2.5pt_{0}({K})
are curl-free and at most affine. Thus (
4.24
) is well-posed.

Unfortunately, in general
\tilde{p}_{h}
from Definition
4.16
does not lie in
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
; if this was the case, then, actually, by Proposition
4.4
or Theorem
4.9
,
\tilde{p}_{h}
would be the weak solution
p
. An illustration of a prototypical situation is provided in Figure
12
, left. For this reason, we continue with a second step, in the spirit of averaging operators in Durán and Padra
[
112
]
, Achdou
et al.

[
3
]
, Karakashian and Pascal
[
157
]
, Ainsworth
[
12
]
, or Burman and Ern
[
68
]
. Recall the Lagrange spaces and nodes of Section
3.5.2
and Figure
4
.

Definition 4.17

(Potential reconstruction by averaging)
.

Let
\tilde{p}_{h}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathcal{T}_{h})
be given by Definition
4.16
. We call a potential reconstruction by averaging a piecewise quadratic and continuous polynomial
\zeta_{h}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathcal{T}_{h})\cap H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
that is prescribed by its values in the Lagrange nodes of the space
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathcal{T}_{h})\cap H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
by

\displaystyle\zeta_{h}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{T}_{\bm{x}}|}\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{\bm{x}}}\tilde{p}_{h}|_{K}(\bm{x})\qquad

\displaystyle\bm{x}\text{ is a Lagrange node of ${\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathcal{T}_{h})\cap H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)$ included in }\Omega,

(4.25a)

\displaystyle\zeta_{h}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle:=0

\displaystyle\bm{x}\text{ is a Lagrange node of ${\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathcal{T}_{h})\cap H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)$ included in }\partial\Omega,

(4.25b)

where
\mathcal{T}_{\bm{x}}
denotes the set of elements of the mesh
\mathcal{T}_{h}
that contain the point
\bm{x}
and
|\mathcal{T}_{\bm{x}}|
is the cardinality (number of elements) of this set.

We simply average all the (typically) different values that
\zeta_{h}
takes in the Lagrange nodes. An illustration is given in Figure
12
, right (where the
0
values at the boundary
\partial\Omega
are not imposed).

4.10
A guaranteed a posteriori error estimate

With the above developments, we are now in a position to present a guaranteed a posteriori error estimate for the cell-centered finite volume discretization (
4.19
) of the Poisson equation (
4.1
) on a simplicial mesh
\mathcal{T}_{h}
. We follow
[
249
, Theorem 4.1]
.

Theorem 4.18
(A guaranteed a posteriori error estimate)
.

Let
p\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
be the exact potential given by (
4.2
) and let
\bm{u}
be the exact flux given by (
4.3
). Let the cell-centered finite volume discretization be given by (
4.19
) and let the approximate flux
\bm{u}_{h}\in\bm{\mathcal{R\hskip-1.00006ptT}}\hskip-2.5pt_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{h})\cap\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
be constructed following Definition
4.14
. Let the potential postprocessing
\tilde{p}_{h}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathcal{T}_{h})
be given by Definition
4.16
and the potential reconstruction by averaging
\zeta_{h}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(\mathcal{T}_{h})\cap H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
by Definition
4.17
. Then there holds

\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}\|\leq\eta:=\Biggl\{\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}}\eta_{K}^{2}\Biggr\}^{1/2},

(4.26a)

where

\eta_{K}^{2}:=\|\bm{u}_{h}+\nabla\zeta_{h}\|_{K}^{2}+\frac{h_{K}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\bigg\|f-\frac{(f,1)_{K}}{|{K}|}\bigg\|_{K}^{2}.

(4.26b)

Proof.

If
f
is piecewise constant, then
\bm{u}_{h}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
with
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{u}_{h}=f
from (
4.23
). In this case, we merely employ inequality (
4.8
) from Corollary
4.6
.

In the general case
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, we rather use equality (
4.9
) from Theorem
4.9
. The second term on the right-hand side of (
4.9
) gives rise to
\|\bm{u}_{h}+\nabla\zeta_{h}\|^{2}
. As for the first one, let
\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
with
\|\nabla\varphi\|=1
be fixed. By the Green theorem (
3.3
), the flux reconstruction property (
4.23
), the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, and the Poincaré inequality

\bigg\|\varphi-\frac{(\varphi,1)_{K}}{|{K}|}\bigg\|_{K}\leq\frac{h_{K}}{\pi}\|\nabla\varphi\|_{K}\qquad\varphi\in H^{1}({K}),

(4.27)

cf.
[
212
,
37
]
, we see

\displaystyle(f,\varphi)+(\bm{u}_{h},\nabla\varphi)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq_Green_Hdv}}}{{=}}(f-\nabla{\cdot}\bm{u}_{h},\varphi)=\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}}(f-\nabla{\cdot}\bm{u}_{h},\varphi)_{K}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq_fr_Pois_prop}}}{{=}}\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}}\bigg(f-\frac{(f,1)_{K}}{|{K}|},\varphi\bigg)_{K}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}}\bigg(f-\frac{(f,1)_{K}}{|{K}|},\varphi-\frac{(\varphi,1)_{K}}{|{K}|}\bigg)_{K}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq_Poinc}}}{{\leq}}\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}}\bigg(\bigg\|f-\frac{(f,1)_{K}}{|{K}|}\bigg\|_{K}\frac{h_{K}}{\pi}\|\nabla\varphi\|_{K}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\Bigg\{\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}}\bigg(\frac{h_{K}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\bigg\|f-\frac{(f,1)_{K}}{|{K}|}\bigg\|_{K}^{2}\bigg)\Bigg\}^{1/2}\underbrace{\|\nabla\varphi\|}_{=1}=\Bigg\{\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}}\bigg(\frac{h_{K}^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\bigg\|f-\frac{(f,1)_{K}}{|{K}|}\bigg\|_{K}^{2}\bigg)\Bigg\}^{1/2}.

Thus (
4.26
) follows. ∎

Remark 4.19

(Local efficiency)
.

Local efficiency of the estimate of (
4.26
) as per Section
2.5.2
holds true, see
[
249
, Theorem 4.2]
.

Remark 4.20

(Rectangular meshes)
.

All the above results are in
[
249
]
also presented on rectangular meshes.

4.11
Numerical experiments

In this Section, we illustrate the application of the a posteriori error estimates of the form of Theorem
4.10
to the finite volume method on two test cases in two space dimensions.

4.11.1
Regular solution

In this test, we consider a model problem from
[
192
]
of form (
4.1
) with
\Omega=(0,1)\times(0,1)
and the load term
f
given such that the (regular exact solution reads

p(x,y)=\frac{10^{2}}{4}x(1-x)y(1-y)\exp\left(-100\left((x-0.75)^{2}+(y-0.75)^{2}\right)\right).

(4.28)

This solution is regular but contains a localized exponential peak.
The numerical tests are performed on a sequence of uniformly refined rectangular meshes
\mathcal{T}_{0},\mathcal{T}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{T}_{J}
,
J=3
, cf. Remarks
3.12
and
4.20
. Figure
13
illustrates the approximate solution given by the values
p_{K}
,
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
, the elementwise errors
\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}\|_{K}
, and the corresponding a posteriori error estimators
\eta_{K}
arising from Theorem
4.10
on mesh
\mathcal{T}_{3}
. We observe that the error is localized in a circular zone around the peak and that the actual and predicted error distributions match very closely.

Figure 13:
[Section
4.11.1
, mesh
\mathcal{T}_{3}
] The approximate solution given by the values
p_{K}
,
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
, (
left
), the exact errors
\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}\|_{K}
(
middle
), and the error estimators
\eta_{K}
from Theorem
4.10
(
right
).

In the left part of Figure
14
, we plot the relative exact errors
\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}\|{/\|\bm{u}_{h}\|}
on each of the meshes
\mathcal{T}_{0}
–
\mathcal{T}_{3}
together with their relative a posteriori error estimates
\eta/\|\bm{u}_{h}\|
from Theorem
4.10
, with respect to the number of mesh elements/number of unknowns. We indeed observe that the estimators give a guaranteed upper bound on the errors. In the right part of Figure
14
we then plot the effectivity indices defined as per (
2.22
) by the ratio of the estimator to the error,

I_{\mathrm{eff}}:=\frac{\eta}{\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}\|}.

(4.29)

We observe that they are remarkably close to one and tend to the optimal value of one with mesh refinement, showing asymptotic exactness as per Section
2.5.4
.

4.11.2
Singular solution

We consider a benchmark test case defined on the L-shaped domain

\Omega=(-1,1)\times(-1,1)\setminus(-1,0]\times(-1,0]

with the exact solution

\displaystyle p(r,\theta)=r^{\frac{2}{3}}\sin\left(\frac{2}{3}\theta+\frac{3}{2}\pi\right),

where (
r,\theta
) are the polar coordinates. The Dirichlet boundary condition is partly inhomogeneous here and given by the value of the exact solution on
\partial\Omega
. We illustrate in Figure
15
the approximate solution given by the values
p_{K}
,
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
, the elementwise errors
\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}\|_{K}
, and the corresponding a posteriori error estimators
\eta_{K}
from Theorem
4.10
. We observe that the a posteriori error estimators
\eta_{K}
detect perfectly the error related in the vicinity of the singularity at
(0,0)
.

(

are depicted in the left part of Figure

. The right part of Figure
16
then displays the corresponding effectivity indices from (
4.29
). We see that, for this test case, the effectivity indices are still close to the optimal value of one, even with singular solution, though no asymptotic exactness is observed here.
