Figure 22:
Relative error
\frac{\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}\|}{\|\bm{u}_{h}\|}
and relative estimators and
\frac{\eta}{\|\bm{u}_{h}\|}
(left) and effectivity indices (right), adaptive mesh refinement

Figure
22
shows the results for adaptive mesh refinement,
achieved by using the local distribution of the predicted error as an
indicator to refine only the cells of the mesh where the error is important. More precisely, we refine the cells
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}
such that
\eta_{K}\geq 0.7\max_{{L}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}}\eta_{L}
.
We observe quasi-identical values of the polygonal MFE and
polygonal HFV estimates; consequently, we obtain the same number of unknowns
at each step of adaptivity and almost identical final energy error for these
two estimators. The left part of Figure
22
displays the
results where we present the estimators and two energy errors, one resulting
with adaptivity based on the polygonal estimates and another with adaptivity
based on the triangular estimates. Almost undistinguishable results are obtained. Finally, adaptive mesh
refinement leads to both smaller error and better effectivity indices in
comparison with uniform mesh refinement.

5.11
Bibliographic resources

Literature on a posteriori analysis on polytopal meshes is much less plentiful than that discussed in Section
4.12
for simplicial meshes. Beirão da Veiga
[
42
]
and Beirão da Veiga and Manzini
[
43
]
derive a posteriori error estimates for low-order mimetic finite difference methods; extensions are presented in Antonietti
et al.

[
22
]
. Omnes
et al.

[
206
]
considered the discrete duality finite volume method.
Recent contributions on the subject include Munar
et al.

[
193
]
or Li
et al.

[
180
]
, see also the references therein.
Here we have followed Vohralík and Yousef
[
255
]
, developing the idea of the local Neumann problems (
5.14
) from Vohralík
[
249
, Section 3.2]
.
Numerous additional results were obtained for more complex model problems, which we discuss in Section
7.12
below.

6
Steady nonlinear pure diffusion problems. Iterative linearization and algebraic errors

As a passage between the model steady linear Darcy flow of
Section
5
and the complex multiphase compositional Darcy flow
of Section
7
, we consider here a steady nonlinear Darcy flow.
We showcase that our methodology applies here in a similar simple fashion as
in Section
5
. We in particular give all the details how inexact
linearization and linear solvers can be taken into account, following the
concept in Ern and Vohralík
[
124
]
, giving rise to
a simple
adaptive inexact Newton method
on
polytopal meshes
. Its
particularity is that it gives a guaranteed upper bound on the total error in
the fluxes on
each linearization
and
each algebraic
solver
step
,
distinguishes the
different error components
, and is obtained by
simple multiplications
of the same element matrices as in the linear case by
vectors of face normal fluxes and potential values immediately available in
each lowest-order locally conservative polytopal scheme.

6.1
Singlephase steady nonlinear Darcy flow

For simplicity of exposition, let us consider the following quasi-linear
version of (
5.1
): find
p:\Omega{\mathbb{R}}
such that

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\nabla{\cdot}({\bm{\underline{K}}}(|\nabla p|)\nabla p)

\displaystyle=f\qquad\mbox{ in }\,\Omega,

(6.1a)

\displaystyle\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=0\qquad\mbox{ on }\,\partial\Omega.

(6.1b)

We suppose that the nonlinearity can be inverted in the sense that

\bm{v}=-{\bm{\underline{K}}}(|\bm{w}|)\bm{w}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\bm{w}=-{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}(|\bm{v}|)\bm{v}

(6.2)

for all
\bm{v},\bm{w}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
We suppose
strong monotonicity
and
Lipschitz-continuity
, i.e.,
that there exist two positive constants
c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}},C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}
so that for all

\bm{v},\bm{w}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,

c_
~

K

—
v
-
w
—^2 ≤(
v
-
w
) ⋅(
~

K

(—
v
—)
v
-
~

K

(—
w
—)
w
),

—
~

K

(—
v
—)
v
-
~

K

(—
w
—)
w
— ≤C_
~

K

—
v
-
w
—.

\ea

Moreover, we let
{\bm{\underline{K}}}
and
{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}
take symmetric values and, namely to
arrive at a simple matrix-vector multiplication form of the estimates, we
also suppose that, for all
\bm{v},\bm{w}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,

c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}|\bm{v}|^{2}\leq\bm{v}{\cdot}{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}(|\bm{w}|)\bm{v},\qquad|{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}(|\bm{w}|)\bm{v}|\leq C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}|\bm{v}|.

(6.4)

Note that for a linear problem,
{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}={\bm{\underline{K}}}^{-1}
,
c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}
is simply the
smallest eigenvalue of
{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}
, i.e., the reciprocal of the largest eigenvalue
of
{\bm{\underline{K}}}
, and
C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}
is the largest eigenvalue of
{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}
, i.e., the
reciprocal of the smallest eigenvalue of
{\bm{\underline{K}}}
.

A prototypical example satisfying (
6.3
)–(
6.4
) is, for two positive constant
c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}
,
C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}
,

\tilde{k}(r):=c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}+\frac{C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}-c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}}{\sqrt{1+r^{2}}},\qquad{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}:=\tilde{k}{\bm{\underline{I}}}.

(6.5)

In this case, it can be easily verified that

c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}\leq\tilde{k}(r)\leq C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}},\qquad c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}\leq(\tilde{k}(r)r)^{\prime}\leq C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}.

An illustration is provided in Figure
23
.
A much wider class of nonlinearities is considered below in Section
7.1
.

Figure 23:
Prototypical monotonous and Lipschitz-continuous function
\tilde{k}(r):=c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}+\frac{C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}-c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}}{\sqrt{1+r^{2}}}
for
c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}=1
and different values of
C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}
(left),
\tilde{k}(r)r
(middle), and
(\tilde{k}(r)r)^{\prime}
(right)

6.2
Weak solution and its properties

In a generalization of Section
5.2
, the weak formulation of (
6.1
) looks for
p\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
such that

({\bm{\underline{K}}}(|\nabla p|)\nabla p,\nabla v)=(f,v)\qquad\forall v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega).

(6.6)

From the pressure head
p
, the Darcy velocity is given by

\bm{u}:=-{\bm{\underline{K}}}(|\nabla p|)\nabla p,

(6.7)

and

\nabla p=-{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}(|\bm{u}|)\bm{u}

(6.8)

from (
6.2
).
Still as in Proposition
4.1
, it follows from (
6.6
) that

\bm{u}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega),\quad\nabla{\cdot}\bm{u}=f.

(6.9)

6.3
Total error and its spatial discretization, linearization, and algebraic components

In the Poisson model problem in Section
4
, we measure the error as
\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}\|
, see (
4.5
). In the Darcy problem in Section
5
, the intrinsic error measure becomes
\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}\|_{{\bm{\underline{K}}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}}
, see (
5.5
). From the mathematical setting in Section
6.1
, a natural extension is here to consider

c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{1/2}\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\|

(6.10)

as the error, where the weight
c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}
in front of the
L^{2}(\Omega)
-norm is the monotonicity constant from (
6.3
).

In (
6.10
), we have denoted the approximate solution by
\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}
, anticipating that our numerical approximation will here consist in
spatial discretization
(with characteristic mesh size
h
),
iterative linearization
(with iteration index
k
), and
iterative algebraic resolution
(with iteration index
i
). Our goal will be to
distinguish the error components
as in (
2.14
) in Section
2.4.6
and to design an a posteriori error estimate of the form (
2.23a
), following the general concepts outlined in Section
2.5
.

6.4
Generic discretizations on a polytopal mesh, iterative linearization, and iterative algebraic system solution

Proceeding as generally as possible, we accommodate any discretization of the form of
Assumption
5.1
. Let
\mathsf{F}_{K}:=(f,1)_{K}
for all
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}
, as in (
5.7
). For the model nonlinear problem (
6.1
), the flux
balance (
5.8
) now takes the form: find the algebraic vector
\mathsf{P}:=\{\mathsf{P}_{K}\}_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|\mathcal{T}_{H}|}
such that

\boxed{\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}}(\mathsf{U}(\mathsf{P}))_{\sigma}\bm{n}_{K}{\cdot}\bm{n}_{\sigma}=\mathsf{F}_{K}\quad\forall{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}.}

(6.11)

Here
(\mathsf{U}(\mathsf{P}))_{\sigma}\in{\mathbb{R}}
for each face
{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{H}
again approximates the normal flux
\langle\bm{u}{\cdot}\bm{n}_{\sigma},1\rangle_{\sigma}
over the face
{\sigma}
, but it depends in a
nonlinear way
on the pressures
\mathsf{P}
, which is denoted by
\mathsf{U}(\mathsf{P})
. It follows that
(
6.11
) is a system of
|\mathcal{T}_{H}|

nonlinear algebraic equations

for the
|\mathcal{T}_{H}|
unknowns
\mathsf{P}
.

The solution to (
6.11
) is typically sought by an
iterative linearization
such as the Newton or the fixed point method. Given an initial guess
\mathsf{P}^{0}
, on each linearization step
k\geq 1
, this leads to a system of
linear algebraic equations
of the form: find the algebraic vector
\mathsf{P}^{k}:=\{\mathsf{P}^{k}_{K}\}_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|\mathcal{T}_{H}|}
such that

\boxed{\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}}(\mathsf{U}^{k-1}(\mathsf{P}^{k}))_{\sigma}\bm{n}_{K}{\cdot}\bm{n}_{\sigma}=\mathsf{F}_{K}\quad\forall{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}.}

(6.12)

Here,
\mathsf{U}^{k-1}(\mathsf{P}^{k})
are linearized face normal fluxes.
These are obtained from the previous iterate
\mathsf{P}^{k-1}
and
\mathsf{U}(\mathsf{P^{k-1}})
but depend in a linear (affine) way on
\mathsf{P}^{k}
. For the Newton linearization, in particular,

(\mathsf{U}^{k-1}(\mathsf{P}^{k}))_{\sigma}:=\sum_{{K}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}}\frac{\partial(\mathsf{U}(\mathsf{P}^{k-1}))_{\sigma}}{\partial\mathsf{P}_{{K}^{\prime}}}\cdot\big(\mathsf{P}^{k}_{{K}^{\prime}}-\mathsf{P}^{k-1}_{{K}^{\prime}}\big)+(\mathsf{U}(\mathsf{P^{k-1}}))_{\sigma}.

(6.13)

In Section
7
below, (
7.27
) provides a general Newton linearization for a more involved problem.

The solution to (
6.12
) is typically sought by an
iterative algebraic solver
. Given an initial guess
\mathsf{P}^{k,0}
, typically the last algebraic solver iterate on the previous linearization step
k-1
when
k>1
, on each algebraic solver step
i\geq 1
, the constant

(\mathsf{R})_{K}^{k,i}:=\mathsf{F}_{K}-\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}}(\mathsf{U}^{k-1}(\mathsf{P}^{k,i}))_{\sigma}\bm{n}_{K}{\cdot}\bm{n}_{\sigma}

(6.14)

is the
algebraic residual
on the element
{K}
,
the misfit of linearized fluxes
\mathsf{U}^{k-1}(\mathsf{P}^{k,i})
not to
satisfy (
6.12
). Consequently, the flux balance on linearization step
k
and algebraic step
i
takes the form

\boxed{\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}}(\mathsf{U}^{k-1}(\mathsf{P}^{k,i}))_{\sigma}\bm{n}_{K}{\cdot}\bm{n}_{\sigma}=\mathsf{F}_{K}-(\mathsf{R})_{K}^{k,i}\quad\forall{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}{,}}

(6.15)

i.e., (
6.12
) up to the algebraic residual.
In order to estimate the algebraic error, we
will, following
[
140
,
154
,
124
]
, also employ
j\geq 1
additional algebraic solver steps
in (
6.15
), giving rise to

\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}}(\mathsf{U}^{k-1}(\mathsf{P}^{k,i+j}))_{\sigma}\bm{n}_{K}{\cdot}\bm{n}_{\sigma}=\mathsf{F}_{K}-(\mathsf{R})_{K}^{k,i+j}\quad\forall{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}.

(6.16)

The motivation is to take
j
large enough to make the algebraic residual
(\mathsf{R})_{K}^{k,i+j}
considerably smaller than
(\mathsf{R})_{K}^{k,i}
, see Remark
6.3
below.

Example 6.1

(Discretization of problem (
6.1
) by the finite volume scheme on a simplicial mesh with fixed-point linearization and an arbitrary iterative algebraic solver)
.

Consider the setting and notation of Section
4.7
.
The cell-centered finite volume scheme for the nonlinear Darcy problem (
6.1
) on the simplicial mesh
\mathcal{T}_{h}
reads: find the real values
p_{K}
,
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
, the approximations to the mean values of
p
in the mesh elements
{K}
, such that

\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}}{\xi}_{{K},{\sigma}}U_{{K},{\sigma}}=(f,1)_{K}\qquad\forall{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h},

(6.17a)

where the real number
{\xi}_{{K},{\sigma}}U_{{K},{\sigma}}
for each face
{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}
approximates the normal (out)flux
\langle\bm{u}{\cdot}\bm{n}_{K},1\rangle_{\sigma}=\langle(-{\bm{\underline{K}}}(|\nabla p|)\nabla p){\cdot}\bm{n}_{K},1\rangle_{\sigma}
from
{K}
over the face
{\sigma}
. This is composed of the approximation of
\langle-\nabla p{\cdot}\bm{n}_{K},1\rangle_{\sigma}
given, as in (
4.19b
)–(
4.19c
), by

\displaystyle U_{{K},{\sigma}}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{|{\sigma}_{{K},{L}}|}{d_{{K},{L}}}(p_{L}-p_{K})\qquad

\displaystyle{\sigma}={\sigma}_{{K},{L}}\in\mathcal{F}_{h}^{\mathrm{int}},

(6.17b)

\displaystyle U_{{K},{\sigma}}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{|{\sigma}|}{d_{{K},{\sigma}}}(0-p_{K})

\displaystyle{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}\cap\mathcal{F}_{h}^{\mathrm{ext}}

(6.17c)

and of the approximation of
{\bm{\underline{K}}}(|\nabla p|)
on
{\sigma}
given by

\displaystyle{\xi}_{{K},{\sigma}}

\displaystyle:={\bm{\underline{K}}}\left(\left|\frac{1}{2}({{\bm{\xi}}}_{h}|_{K}(\bm{x}_{{K},{\sigma}})+{{\bm{\xi}}}_{h}|_{L}(\bm{x}_{{K},{\sigma}}))\right|\right)\qquad

\displaystyle{\sigma}={\sigma}_{{K},{L}}\in\mathcal{F}_{h}^{\mathrm{int}},

(6.17d)

\displaystyle{\xi}_{{K},{\sigma}}

\displaystyle:={\bm{\underline{K}}}(|{{\bm{\xi}}}_{h}|_{K}(\bm{x}_{{K},{\sigma}})|)

\displaystyle{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}\cap\mathcal{F}_{h}^{\mathrm{ext}},

(6.17e)

where the approximation of
-\nabla p
is, as in (
4.22
), given by

({{\bm{\xi}}}_{h}|_{K})(\bm{x}):=\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}}U_{{K},{\sigma}}\frac{1}{d|{K}|}(\bm{x}-{\bm{a}}_{{K},{\sigma}}),\qquad\bm{x}\in{K},\,{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h},

(6.17f)

see the illustrations in Figure
17
.
Note that in contrast to (
4.19b
)–(
4.19c
), the face fluxes
{\xi}_{{K},{\sigma}}U_{{K},{\sigma}}
are now nonlinear functions of the pressure unknowns
p_{K}
and
p_{L}
(unless
{\bm{\underline{K}}}({\cdot})
is a constant function). Also note that (
6.17
) writes in the form (
6.11
) with
(\mathsf{U}(\mathsf{P}))_{\sigma}={\xi}_{{K},{\sigma}}U_{{K},{\sigma}}\bm{n}_{K}{\cdot}\bm{n}_{\sigma}
(recall that
\bm{n}_{K}{\cdot}\bm{n}_{\sigma}=\pm 1
only determines the sign).

Iterative linearization of (
6.17
) by the fixed point method then reads: given the initial values
p_{K}^{0}
,
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
, on each linearization step
k\geq 1
, find the real values
p_{K}^{k}
,
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
, such that

\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}}{\xi}_{{K},{\sigma}}^{k-1}U_{{K},{\sigma}}^{k}=(f,1)_{K}\qquad\forall{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h},

(6.18a)

where

\displaystyle U_{{K},{\sigma}}^{k}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{|{\sigma}_{{K},{L}}|}{d_{{K},{L}}}(p_{L}^{k}-p_{K}^{k})\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle{\sigma}={\sigma}_{{K},{L}}\in\mathcal{F}_{h}^{\mathrm{int}},

(6.18b)

\displaystyle U_{{K},{\sigma}}^{k}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{|{\sigma}|}{d_{{K},{\sigma}}}(0-p_{K}^{k})

\displaystyle{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}\cap\mathcal{F}_{h}^{\mathrm{ext}},

(6.18c)

\displaystyle{\xi}_{{K},{\sigma}}^{k-1}

\displaystyle:={\bm{\underline{K}}}\left(\left|\frac{1}{2}({{\bm{\xi}}}_{h}^{k-1}|_{K}(\bm{x}_{{K},{\sigma}})+{{\bm{\xi}}}_{h}^{k-1}|_{L}(\bm{x}_{{K},{\sigma}}))\right|\right)

\displaystyle{\sigma}={\sigma}_{{K},{L}}\in\mathcal{F}_{h}^{\mathrm{int}},

(6.18d)

\displaystyle{\xi}_{{K},{\sigma}}^{k-1}

\displaystyle:={\bm{\underline{K}}}(|{{\bm{\xi}}}_{h}^{k-1}|_{K}(\bm{x}_{{K},{\sigma}})|)

\displaystyle{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}\cap\mathcal{F}_{h}^{\mathrm{ext}},

(6.18e)

\displaystyle({{\bm{\xi}}}_{h}^{k-1}|_{K})(\bm{x})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}}U_{{K},{\sigma}}^{k-1}\frac{1}{d|{K}|}(\bm{x}-{\bm{a}}_{{K},{\sigma}}),

\displaystyle\bm{x}\in{K},\,{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}.

(6.18f)

We see that (
6.18
) takes the form requested in (
6.12
).

Finally, (
6.15
) for an arbitrary iterative algebraic solver follows by the definition (
6.14
).

6.5
Face normal fluxes and potential point values

The key for the guaranteed a posteriori error estimate with inexpensive implementation and evaluation for the linear Darcy problem (
5.1
) in Theorem
5.9
were the face normal fluxes of Section
5.5
and the potential point values of Section
5.7
.
We will now identify the face normal fluxes and potential point values for the present nonlinear Darcy problem (
6.1
). In order to devise a guaranteed a posteriori error estimate distinguishing the error components, we will also identify
linearization error
and
algebraic error
face fluxes.

Let a polytopal cell
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}
with its face
{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}
be fixed.
For a given linearization step
k\geq 1
, algebraic step
i\geq 1
, and
j\geq 1
additional algebraic iterations, we set

\displaystyle(\mathsf{U}_{{K}}^{{k,i}})_{\sigma}

\displaystyle:=(\mathsf{U}(\mathsf{P}^{k,i}))_{\sigma},

(6.19a)

\displaystyle(\mathsf{U}_{{\rm lin},{K}}^{{k,i}})_{\sigma}

\displaystyle:=(\mathsf{U}^{k-1}(\mathsf{P}^{k,i}))_{\sigma}-(\mathsf{U}(\mathsf{P}^{k,i}))_{\sigma},

(6.19b)

\displaystyle(\mathsf{U}_{{\rm alg},{K}}^{{k,i}})_{\sigma}

\displaystyle:=(\mathsf{U}^{k-1}(\mathsf{P}^{k,i+j}))_{\sigma}-(\mathsf{U}^{k-1}(\mathsf{P}^{k,i}))_{\sigma}.

(6.19c)

Observe that
\mathsf{U}^{k-1}(\mathsf{P}^{k,i+j})
are directly available as employed in (
6.16
) and that
\mathsf{U}^{k-1}(\mathsf{P}^{k,i})
are directly available as employed in (
6.15
). In turn,
\mathsf{U}(\mathsf{P}^{k,i})
are available upon plugging the current approximation
\mathsf{P}^{k,i}
into the nonlinear flux function of (
6.11
).
We call
\mathsf{U}_{{K}}^{{k,i}}

discretization face fluxes
since in limit
k,i\infty
, they tend to
\mathsf{U}(\mathsf{P})
of (
6.11
).
Similarly, we call
\mathsf{U}_{{\rm lin},{K}}^{{k,i}}

linearization error face fluxes
since they vanish with linearization and algebraic solver iterations
k{,i}\infty
.
Finally, we call
\mathsf{U}_{{\rm alg},{K}}^{{k,i}}
the
algebraic error face fluxes
since they vanish with algebraic solver iterations
i\infty
.
Observe that

(\mathsf{U}_{{K}}^{{k,i}})_{\sigma}+(\mathsf{U}_{{\rm lin},{K}}^{{k,i}})_{\sigma}+(\mathsf{U}_{{\rm alg},{K}}^{{k,i}})_{\sigma}=(\mathsf{U}^{k-1}(\mathsf{P}^{k,i+j}))_{\sigma},

so that from (
6.16
)

\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}}\left((\mathsf{U}_{{K}}^{{k,i}})_{\sigma}+(\mathsf{U}_{{\rm lin},{K}}^{{k,i}})_{\sigma}+(\mathsf{U}_{{\rm alg},{K}}^{{k,i}})_{\sigma}\right)\bm{n}_{K}{\cdot}\bm{n}_{\sigma}=\mathsf{F}_{K}-(\mathsf{R})_{K}^{k,i+j}\quad\forall{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}.

(6.20)

The fluxes per simplex face are then obtained as in Definition
5.3
.

The potential point values are obtained as in Definition
5.11
or
5.13
directly from the cell pressure heads
\mathsf{P}^{k,i}
.
This gives the vectors
\mathsf{Z}_{K}^{k,i}
and
\mathsf{Z}_{K}^{k,i,\mathrm{ext}}
of
potential point values
in the vertices of the simplicial submesh
\mathcal{T}_{K}
of each polytopal element
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}
.

6.6
Fictitious flux and potential reconstructions

We now extend the reconstructions of Sections
5.6
and
5.8
to the present nonlinear setting.

We have to face the fact that the diffusion tensor
{\bm{\underline{K}}}(|\nabla p|)
is now unknown since
p
is unknown. We thus modify Definition
5.5
to

\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}{|_{K}}:=c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{-1}C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{2}\arg\min_{\bm{v}_{h}\in\bm{V}_{h,\mathrm{N}}^{K},\,\nabla{\cdot}\bm{v}_{h}={\mathrm{constant}}}\|\bm{v}_{h}\|_{{K}}^{2},

(6.21)

where the data in the spaces
\bm{V}_{h,\mathrm{N}}^{K}
are now given by
\mathsf{U}_{{K}}^{{k,i}}
from (
6.19a
) in place of
\mathsf{U}_{\sigma}
(we systematically use the convention (
5.12
) (without change of the notation) to define the fluxes of the simplicial submesh faces in case they subdivide the original polytopal faces). Note that we cannot in general impose here
\nabla{\cdot}(\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i})|_{K}=f|_{K}
. This
discretization flux reconstruction
is again not needed to evaluate the a posteriori error estimators of Theorem
6.7
below (it would, however, be needed to evaluate the error
c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{1/2}\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\|
.) For appropriate scaling, we have replaced
{\bm{\underline{K}}}^{-1}
by
c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{-1}C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{2}{\bm{\underline{I}}}
.
It is important to stress that just like in (
5.16
), problem (
6.21
)
is
linear
and can be written in a matrix form as
in (
5.18
), with the
same matrices
, just replacing
{\bm{\underline{K}}}^{-1}
by
c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{-1}C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{2}{\bm{\underline{I}}}
. This in particular leads to the definition of the MFE element matrix
\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{\mathrm{MFE},{K}}
by (
5.20
) also in the present nonlinear setting, up to the replacement of
{\bm{\underline{K}}}^{-1}
by
c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{-1}C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{2}{\bm{\underline{I}}}
.

Similarly, we define the linearization error flux reconstruction
\bm{u}_{\mathrm{lin},h}^{k,i}
by the lifting (
6.21
), employing
\mathsf{U}_{{\rm lin},{K}}^{{k,i}}
from (
6.19b
) in the definition of the space
\bm{V}_{h,\mathrm{N}}^{K}
, and the algebraic error flux reconstruction
\bm{u}_{\mathrm{alg},h}^{k,i}
by the lifting (
6.21
), employing
\mathsf{U}_{{\rm alg},{K}}^{{k,i}}
from (
6.19c
) in the definition of the space
\bm{V}_{h,\mathrm{N}}^{K}
.
The flux reconstructions
\bm{u}_{\mathrm{lin},h}^{k,i}
and
\bm{u}_{\mathrm{alg},h}^{k,i}
will only be used as a theoretical vehicle below.
From (
6.20
), we observe by the Green theorem that

{\big(}\nabla{\cdot}\big(\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}+\bm{u}_{\mathrm{lin},h}^{k,i}+\bm{u}_{\mathrm{alg},h}^{k,i}\big){\big)|_{K}}=f|_{K}-|{K}|^{-1}(\mathsf{R})_{K}^{k,i+j}\quad\forall{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}.

(6.22)

Finally, the
potential reconstruction

\zeta_{h}^{k,i}
is simply obtained from the element vectors
\mathsf{Z}_{K}^{k,i}
and
\mathsf{Z}_{K}^{k,i,\mathrm{ext}}
assembled in Section
6.5
as in Definition
5.14
, i.e., setting

\zeta_{h}^{k,i}({\bm{a}}):=\mathsf{Z}_{{\bm{a}}}^{k,i}\qquad\forall{\bm{a}}\in\mathcal{V}_{h}.

Congruently, for any
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}
, we define the stiffness matrix
\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{\mathrm{FE},{K}}
as in (
5.32
), with
c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{-2}C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}{\bm{\underline{I}}}
in place of
{\bm{\underline{K}}}
, whereas the mass matrix
\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{FE},{K}}
is defined exactly as in (
5.33
).

6.7
A guaranteed a posteriori error estimate distinguishing the error
components with inexpensive implementation and evaluation

Let
C_{\mathrm{F}}
be the constant from the Friedrichs inequality

\|v\|\leq C_{\mathrm{F}}h_{\Omega}\|\nabla v\|\qquad\forall v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega);

(6.23)

there holds
1/(\pi d)\leq C_{\mathrm{F}}\leq 1
, as
C_{\mathrm{F}}h_{\Omega}
is the square root of the reciprocal of the smallest eigenvalue of the
Laplace operator on
\Omega
with homogenous Dirichlet boundary condition. The
main result of this section is:

Theorem 6.2
(A guaranteed a posteriori error estimate distinguishing the error
components with inexpensive implementation and evaluation)
.

Let
f
be constant on each
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}
and let
\bm{u}
be given by (
6.6
)–(
6.7
). Consider any polytopal
discretization of the form (
6.11
), any
iterative linearization (
6.12
) on step
k\geq 1
, and any
iterative algebraic solver (
6.15
) on step
i\geq 1
.
Consider
j\geq 1
additional algebraic solver steps leading to (
6.16
).
Let, for each polytopal element
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}
, the element vectors of face normal fluxes
\mathsf{U}_{{K}}^{{k,i}}
,
\mathsf{U}_{{\rm lin},{K}}^{{k,i}}
, and
\mathsf{U}_{{\rm alg},{K}}^{{k,i}}
be given by (
6.19
).
Let the element vectors of potential point values
\mathsf{Z}_{K}^{k,i}
and
\mathsf{Z}_{K}^{k,i,\mathrm{ext}}
be constructed from the values
\mathsf{P}^{k,i}
following Section
6.5
.
Let finally the element matrices
\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{\mathrm{MFE},{K}}
and

\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{\mathrm{FE},{K}}
be defined by (
5.20
)
and (
5.32
), where one respectively takes the multiples of the
identity matrix
c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{-1}C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{2}{\bm{\underline{I}}}
in place of
{\bm{\underline{K}}}^{-1}
and

c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{-2}C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}{\bm{\underline{I}}}
in place of
{\bm{\underline{K}}}
. Let also

\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{FE},{K}}
be given
by (
5.33
). Then there holds

c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{1/2}\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\|\leq\eta_{{\rm sp}}^{k,i}+\eta_{{\rm lin}}^{k,i}+\eta_{{\rm alg}}^{k,i}+\eta_{{\rm rem}}^{k,i}

(6.24)

with

\eta_{{\rm\bullet}}^{k,i}=\left\{\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}}\left(\eta_{{\rm\bullet},{K}}^{k,i}\right)^{2}\right\}^{1/2},\qquad\bullet=\{\mathrm{sp},\,\mathrm{lin},\,\mathrm{alg},\,\mathrm{rem}\},

where the (spatial)
discretization estimators
are given by

\left(\eta_{{\rm sp},{K}}^{k,i}\right)^{2}\!:=\big(\mathsf{U}_{{K}}^{{k,i}}\big)^{\mathrm{t}}\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{\mathrm{MFE},{K}}\mathsf{U}_{{K}}^{{k,i}}+(\mathsf{Z}_{K}^{k,i})^{\mathrm{t}}\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{\mathrm{FE},{K}}\mathsf{Z}_{K}^{k,i}+2c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{-1}C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}\left[(\mathsf{U}_{K}^{k,i,\mathrm{ext}})^{\mathrm{t}}\mathsf{Z}_{K}^{k,i,\mathrm{ext}}\!-{\mathsf{F}_{K}|{K}|^{-1}}\mathsf{1}^{\mathrm{t}}\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{FE},{K}}\mathsf{Z}_{K}^{k,i}\right]\!,

the
linearization estimators
by

\left(\eta_{{\rm lin},{K}}^{k,i}\right)^{2}:=(\mathsf{U}_{{\rm lin},{K}}^{{k,i}})^{\mathrm{t}}\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{\mathrm{MFE},{K}}\mathsf{U}_{{\rm lin},{K}}^{{k,i}},

the
algebraic estimators
by,

\left(\eta_{{\rm alg},{K}}^{k,i}\right)^{2}:=(\mathsf{U}_{{\rm alg},{K}}^{{k,i}})^{\mathrm{t}}\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{\mathrm{MFE},{K}}\mathsf{U}_{{\rm alg},{K}}^{{k,i}},

and the
algebraic remainder estimators
by

\eta_{{\rm rem},{K}}^{k,i}:=c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{-1/2}C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}C_{\mathrm{F}}h_{\Omega}|{K}|^{-1/2}|(\mathsf{R})_{K}^{k,i+j}|.

Here, the flux reconstruction
\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\in\bm{\mathcal{R\hskip-1.00006ptT}}\hskip-2.5pt_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{h})\cap\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
is obtained following (
6.21
).

Remark 6.3

(Algebraic remainder estimators
\eta_{{\rm rem},{K}}^{k,i}
, choice of
j
additional linear solver iterations, and mass balance)
.

From (
6.20
) or (
6.22
), at “convergence” of the iterative algebraic solver (or for a direct solver)(recall that we neglect rounding errors), as the algebraic residuals
(\mathsf{R})_{K}^{k,i+j}
vanish, we recover exact mass balance on each linearization step
k\geq 1
. More importantly, for a sufficiently high number
j
of additional algebraic iterations, we “almost” have the mass balance on each linearization step
k\geq 1
. We typically choose
j
adaptively, following
[
140
,
154
,
124
]
, so that this mass balance misfit related to the algebraic residuals
(\mathsf{R})_{K}^{k,i+j}
is negligible: we choose
j
such that
\eta_{{\rm rem}}^{k,i}
becomes negligible in comparison with the other estimators in (
6.24
).
The presence of the terms
(\mathsf{R})_{K}^{k,i+j}
is the disadvantage of the
j
additional iterations. In turn, the advantage of the
j
of additional algebraic iterations is the simplicity of the formula (
6.19c
).
A more involved choice can be made following
[
210
]
. Then no
j
additional iterations are needed, the term
|{K}|^{-1}(\mathsf{R})_{K}^{k,i+j}
in (
6.20
) and (
6.22
) and the term
\eta_{{\rm rem}}^{k,i}
in (
6.24
) vanish, there is an exact mass balance on each linearization step
k\geq 1
and each linear algebraic step
i\geq 1
, and the a posteriori bound (
6.24
) is typically slightly more precise. Remark finally that these two choices respectively correspond to nonzero and zero
\rho_{h}^{k,i}
in the general discussion in Section
2.7
.

Proof.

The proof follows in spirit of previous works by
Kim
[
163
]
, Jiránek
et al.

[
154
]
, and Ern and
Vohralík
[
124
,
125
]
. Let
\zeta\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)

be the solution of

({\bm{\underline{K}}}(|\nabla\zeta|)\nabla\zeta,\nabla v)=-(\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i},\nabla v)\qquad\forall v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)

(6.25)

and set
\bm{\zeta}:=-{\bm{\underline{K}}}(|\nabla\zeta|)\nabla\zeta
, similarly to (
6.7
). Remark that if there holds
\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\in\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},\Omega)
with
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{u}_{h}^{{k,i}}=f
(if (
6.11
) is satisfied exactly, no iterative linearization and exact algebraic solve), then, by the Green theorem,
-(\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i},\nabla v)=(f,v)
, so that
\zeta
coincides with the solution
p
of (
6.6
).

By the triangle inequality,

c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{1/2}\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\|\leq c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{1/2}\|\bm{u}-\bm{\zeta}\|+c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{1/2}\|\bm{\zeta}-\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\|.

(6.26)

For the first term above, the strong monotonicity implies

\displaystyle c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}\|\bm{u}-\bm{\zeta}\|^{2}\leq{}

\displaystyle({\bm{\underline{K}}}(|\nabla p|)\nabla p-{\bm{\underline{K}}}(|\nabla\zeta|)\nabla\zeta,\nabla(p-\zeta))

by (
6.3
) and (
6.2
)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle({\bm{\underline{K}}}(|\nabla p|)\nabla p+\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i},\nabla(p-\zeta))

by (
6.25
)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(f,p-\zeta)+(\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i},\nabla(p-\zeta)).

by (
6.6
)

Note that by the Lipschitz-continuity (
6.3
) together
with (
6.2
),

\|\nabla(p-\zeta)\|\leq C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}\|\bm{u}-\bm{\zeta}\|.

(6.27)

Thus,

\begin{split}c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{1/2}\|\bm{u}-\bm{\zeta}\|&=\frac{(f,p-\zeta)+(\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i},\nabla(p-\zeta))}{c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{1/2}\|\bm{u}-\bm{\zeta}\|}\\
&\leq c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{-1/2}C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}\max_{\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega),\,\|\nabla\varphi\|=1}\{(f,\varphi)+(\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i},\nabla\varphi)\}.\end{split}

(6.28)

To estimate (
6.28
) by a computable quantity, we use (
6.22
).
Fix now
\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
with

\|\nabla\varphi\|=1
. Then adding and subtracting

(\bm{u}_{\mathrm{lin},h}^{k,i}+\bm{u}_{\mathrm{alg},h}^{k,i},\nabla\varphi)
and using
the Green theorem,

\displaystyle(f,\varphi)+(\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i},\nabla\varphi)

\displaystyle=(f-\nabla{\cdot}(\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}+\bm{u}_{\mathrm{lin},h}^{k,i}+\bm{u}_{\mathrm{alg},h}^{k,i}),\varphi)-(\bm{u}_{\mathrm{lin},h}^{k,i}+\bm{u}_{\mathrm{alg},h}^{k,i},\nabla\varphi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}}\{|{K}|^{-1}((\mathsf{R})_{K}^{k,i+j},\varphi)_{K}-(\bm{u}_{\mathrm{lin},h}^{k,i}+\bm{u}_{\mathrm{alg},h}^{k,i},\nabla\varphi)_{K}\}.

The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality gives

\displaystyle\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}}-(\bm{u}_{\mathrm{lin},h}^{k,i}+\bm{u}_{\mathrm{alg},h}^{k,i},\nabla\varphi)_{K}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}}\|\bm{u}_{\mathrm{lin},h}^{k,i}+\bm{u}_{\mathrm{alg},h}^{k,i}\|_{K}\|\nabla\varphi\|_{K}\leq\|\bm{u}_{\mathrm{lin},h}^{k,i}+\bm{u}_{\mathrm{alg},h}^{k,i}\|\underbrace{\|\nabla\varphi\|}_{=1}

\displaystyle\leq\|\bm{u}_{\mathrm{lin},h}^{k,i}\|+\|\bm{u}_{\mathrm{alg},h}^{k,i}\|.

Similarly, again the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality leads to

\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}}|{K}|^{-1}|((\mathsf{R})_{K}^{k,i+j},\varphi)_{K}|\leq\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}}|{K}|^{-1/2}|(\mathsf{R})_{K}^{k,i+j}|\|\varphi\|_{K}\leq\left\{\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}}|{K}|^{-1}|(\mathsf{R})_{K}^{k,i+j}|^{2}\right\}^{1/2}\|\varphi\|_{\Omega}.

(6.29)

Summing up these developments with the Friedrichs
inequality (
6.23
), infer from (
6.28
)

c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{1/2}\|\bm{u}-\bm{\zeta}\|\leq c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{-1/2}C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}\left[C_{\mathrm{F}}h_{\Omega}\left\{\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}}|{K}|^{-1}|(\mathsf{R})_{K}^{k,i+j}|^{2}\right\}^{1/2}+\|\bm{u}_{\mathrm{lin},h}^{k,i}\|+\|\bm{u}_{\mathrm{alg},h}^{k,i}\|\right].

Finally, to evaluate the norm
\|\bm{u}_{\mathrm{lin},h}^{k,i}\|
, we use

\|\bm{u}_{\mathrm{lin},h}^{k,i}\|^{2}=\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}}\|\bm{u}_{\mathrm{lin},h}^{k,i}\|_{{K}}^{2}

and employ the constant matrix
c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{-1}C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{2}{\bm{\underline{I}}}
in place of

{\bm{\underline{K}}}^{-1}
in Lemma
5.8
to
infer

c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{-1}C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{2}\|\bm{u}_{\mathrm{lin},h}^{k,i}\|_{K}^{2}=\big(\mathsf{U}_{{\rm lin},{K}}^{{k,i}}\big)^{\mathrm{t}}\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{\mathrm{MFE},{K}}\mathsf{U}_{{\rm lin},{K}}^{{k,i}}

(6.30)

for each polytopal element
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}
. We proceed similarly for
\|\bm{u}_{\mathrm{alg},h}^{k,i}\|
, which gives

c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{-1}C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{2}\|\bm{u}_{\mathrm{alg},h}^{k,i}\|_{K}^{2}=\big(\mathsf{U}_{{\rm alg},{K}}^{{k,i}}\big)^{\mathrm{t}}\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{\mathrm{MFE},{K}}\mathsf{U}_{{\rm alg},{K}}^{{k,i}}.

(6.31)

We finally estimate the second term in (
6.26
). For
v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)

arbitrary, the strong monotonicity in particular leads to

\displaystyle c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}\|\bm{\zeta}-\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq({\bm{\underline{K}}}(|\nabla\zeta|)\nabla\zeta+\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i},\nabla\zeta+{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}(|\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}|)\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i})

by (
6.3
) and (
6.2
)

\displaystyle=({\bm{\underline{K}}}(|\nabla\zeta|)\nabla\zeta+\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i},\nabla v+{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}(|\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}|)\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i})

by (
6.25
)

\displaystyle\leq\|\bm{\zeta}-\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\|\|{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}(|\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}|)\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}+\nabla v\|

by Cauchy–Schwarz

so that,

c_
~

K

^1/2∥
ζ
-
u
_h^k,i∥ ≤c_
~

K

^-1/2min_v ∈H^1_0(Ω) ∥
~

K

(—
u
_h^k,i—)
u
_h^k,i + ∇v∥

≤c_
~

K

^-1/2∥
~

K

(—
u
_h^k,i—)
u
_h^k,i + ∇
ζ
_h^k,i∥

\ea

for an arbitrary
\zeta_{h}^{k,i}\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
. We pick the potential reconstruction from Section
6.6
. For the inexpensive implementation and evaluation, we proceed as in Section
5.9
. Consequently, for each polytopal mesh
element
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}
, employing here also the eigenvalue
hypothesis (
6.4
),

\displaystyle\|{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}(|\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}|)\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}+\nabla\zeta_{h}^{k,i}\|_{{K}}^{2}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\|[{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}(|\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}|)]^{1/2}\big([{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}(|\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}|)]^{1/2}\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}+[{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}(|\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}|)]^{-1/2}\nabla\zeta_{h}^{k,i}\big)\|_{{K}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}\|[{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}(|\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}|)]^{1/2}\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}+[{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}(|\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}|)]^{-1/2}\nabla\zeta_{h}^{k,i}\|_{{K}}^{2}

by (
6.4
)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}\left(({\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}(|\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}|)\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i},\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i})_{K}+2(\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i},\nabla\zeta_{h}^{k,i})_{K}+([{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}(|\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}|)]^{-1}\nabla\zeta_{h}^{k,i},\nabla\zeta_{h}^{k,i})_{K}\right)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{2}\|\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\|_{K}^{2}+2C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}(\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i},\nabla\zeta_{h}^{k,i})_{K}+c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{-1}C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}\|\nabla\zeta_{h}^{k,i}\|_{K}^{2}.

by (
6.4
)

For the first term above, we proceed as in (
6.30
). For the second
one, the development (
5.39
) remains unchanged. Finally, for the
third term, we use (
5.34
) where
{\bm{\underline{K}}}
has been replaced by

c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{-2}C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}{\bm{\underline{I}}}
to see that

c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{-2}C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}\|\nabla\zeta_{h}^{k,i}\|_{K}^{2}=(\mathsf{Z}_{K}^{k,i})^{\mathrm{t}}\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{\mathrm{FE},{K}}\mathsf{Z}_{K}^{k,i}.

The proof is finished by combining the two estimates on
c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{1/2}\|\bm{u}-\bm{\zeta}\|
and
c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{1/2}\|\bm{\zeta}-\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\|
. ∎

Remark 6.4

(Inexpensive implementation and evaluation)
.

Note that the estimate of Theorem
6.7
still takes the
same simple matrix-vector multiplication form of Theorem
5.9
,
with in particular the same element matrices

\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{\mathrm{MFE},{K}}
,

\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{\mathrm{FE},{K}}
,

\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{FE},{K}}
as in the liner case. Similarly to
Corollaries
5.19
and
5.20
, a
further simplification is to replace
\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{\mathrm{MFE},{K}}

by the element matrices
\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{{K}}
of the given scheme whenever they are available, as per Assumption
5.2
.

Remark 6.5

(Distinction of error components)
.

The distinction of the different error components is based on the face fluxes (
6.19
), directly available form any finite volume discretization (
6.11
)–(
6.16
). Beyond simplicity, a remarkable property is that all the error component estimators have the same physical units (that of energy flux error) and take the same form. This stands in contrast to the usual practice where the algebraic error is typically treated by the (relative)
L^{2}
-norm of the algebraic residual (here, from (
6.33
), we rather employ the energy-scaled
H^{-1}(\Omega)
-norm of the residual) and the linearization error estimator is often the
L^{\infty}
-norm of the difference of two consecutive iterates. This unified treatment of the different error components is a consequence of our holistic approach. In practice, the distinction of error components is to be used in an adaptive inexact Newton algorithm such as Algorithm
7.1
of the next section.

Remark 6.6

(Error structure)
.

From (
6.26
),
(
6.28
), and (
6.32
), we see that

\begin{split}c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{1/2}\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\|&\leq c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{-1/2}C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}\max_{\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega),\,\|\nabla\varphi\|=1}\{(f,\varphi)+(\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i},\nabla\varphi)\}+c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{-1/2}\min_{v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)}\|{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}(|\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}|)\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}+\nabla v\|\\
&\leq 2c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{-1/2}C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\|;\end{split}

(6.33)

in the second inequality, we have used the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality
and (
6.8
) together with (
6.3
) for the second term. This means that the energy error

c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{1/2}\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\|
is equivalent to the sum of
the dual norm of the residual and nonconformity evaluated as the distance to the
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
space. This is an immediate extension of Theorem
4.9
from the linear case. Compared to Corollary
4.6
for the linear case with an exact algebraic solver giving
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{u}_{h}=f
, this in particular enables to treat the
nonlinear case with inexact linearization and algebraic solvers, so that
\nabla{\cdot}\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\neq f
. Our motivation here was to evaluate the error as the
\bm{L}^{2}(\Omega)
-norm, which gives rise to the weights
c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}
and
C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}
and the factor
2
in (
6.33
). Alternatively, we could have stick to the intrinsic error measure of the form

\left\{\max_{\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega),\,\|\nabla\varphi\|=1}\{(f,\varphi)+(\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i},\nabla\varphi)\}^{2}+\min_{v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)}\|{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}(|\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}|)\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}+\nabla v\|^{2}\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}},

cf. (
4.9
), which would lead to constant-free estimates without the weights
c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}
and
C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}
. In this way, we define the error measure
\mathcal{N}^{n,k,i}
in (
7.17a
) in the complex multiphase flow below.

6.8
Numerical experiments

In this section, we numerically illustrate the efficiency of our theoretical results of Section
6.7
on two different examples. Our main goals are to asses the sharpness of the guaranteed bound (
6.24
) and to examine the robustness of our estimates with respect to the ratio
C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}/c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}
where we consider
C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}/c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}=10^{i},\,i\in\{1,...,6\}
. As in previous sections, the effectivity index is defined by the ratio of the estimator from Theorem
6.7
to the exact error,

I_{\mathrm{eff}}:=\frac{\eta^{k,i}}{c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{1/2}\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\|}.

(6.34)

Additionally, we study in the following examples a stopping criterion for the linearization algorithm based on balancing the error components of Theorem
6.7
. The criterion is that the linearization iteration is pursued until step
k
such that

\eta_{{\rm lin}}^{k,i}\leq\Gamma_{\rm lin}{\eta_{{\rm sp}}^{k,i}},

(6.35)

with
\Gamma_{\rm lin}\in(0,1)
(we focus on the linearization only, the algebraic solver is used with a very small tolerance yielding
\eta_{{\rm alg}}^{k,i},\eta_{{\rm rem}}^{k,i}\approx 0
). We compare this criterion with a classic one based on a fixed threshold on the relative linearization residual

e_{\rm lin}^{k}\leq 10^{-8},

(6.36)

where the relative linearization residual, for a resolution of a system of nonlinear algebraic equations

F(X)=0
by the Newton method, is given by

e_{\rm lin}^{k}:=\frac{\|F(X^{k})\|}{\|F(X^{0})\|}.

Note that the criterion (
6.35
) can be used in more general adaptive algorithm balancing all the error components such as in Algorithm
7.1
.

6.8.1
Regular solution

In this test, we consider on the domain
\Omega=(0,1)\times(0,1)
the exact solution

p(x,y)=16x(1-x)y(1-y).

(6.37)

The source term
f
is then constructed through (
6.1
). We perform the numerical tests with the nonlinearity example given by (
6.5
) on a uniformly refined rectangular mesh of
10^{4}
elements. Figure
24
shows the approximate solution given by the values
p^{k,i}_{K}
,
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
, the elementwise errors
c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{1/2}\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\|_{K}
, and the corresponding a posteriori error estimators
\eta^{k,i}_{K}
from Theorem
6.7
.

Figure 24:
[Section

] The approximate solution given by the values
p_{K}^{{k,i}}
,
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
, (

) the energy errors
c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{1/2}\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\|_{K}
(

), and the error estimators
\eta_{{\rm\mathrm{sp}},{K}}^{k,i}+\eta_{{\rm\mathrm{lin}},{K}}^{k,i}+\eta_{{\rm\mathrm{alg}},{K}}^{k,i}+\eta_{{\rm\mathrm{rem}},{K}}^{k,i}
from Theorem
6.7
(
right
).

The robustness of our estimates with respect to the ratio
C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}/c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}
is examined in Figure
25
, where we fix the value of
c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}=1
and plot, for different values of
C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}\in\{10,100,...,10^{6}\}
, the values of the relative energy error
c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{1/2}\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\|{/\|\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\|}
and the corresponding relative total error estimate
\eta^{k,i}{/\|\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\|}
from Theorem
6.7
. The effectivity index plotted in the right part of Figure

reveal that, in this case and for the choice of nonlinearity example (

), the estimate is robust and not affected by the values of the ratio
C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}/c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}
.

Figure

is dedicated to study the stopping criterion (

) and its comparison with the usual stopping criterion (
6.36
). The left part of Figure
26
depicts the evolution of the total estimator
\eta^{k,i}
, the linearization estimator
\eta_{\rm lin}^{k,i}
, and the relative linearization residual

as a function of the number of Newton iterations for a uniform fixed mesh of
10^{4}

C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}/c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}=10

6.35
) with
\Gamma_{\rm 1in}=0.1
, indicates that the resolution can be stopped at the sixth iteration, which already yields a sufficiently accurate approximate solution and helps avoid unnecessary additional iterations. The right part of Figure
26
shows a comparison of the required number of Newton iteration in order to satisfy the standard criterion
e_{\rm lin}^{k}\leq 10^{-8}
and the adaptive one
\eta_{{\rm lin}}^{k,i}\leq 0.1{\eta_{{\rm sp}}^{k,i}}
for different values of the ratio
C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}/c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}
. We see that we can save at least the half of Newton iterations on each resolution without altering the precision.

6.8.2
Singular solution

In this test, we consider the L-shaped domain

\Omega=(-1,1)\times(-1,1)\setminus(-1,0]\times(-1,0]

with the exact solution

\displaystyle p(r,\theta)=r^{\frac{2}{3}}\sin\left(\frac{2}{3}\theta+\frac{3}{2}\pi\right),

where (
r,\theta
) are the polar coordinates.
The Dirichlet boundary condition is partly inhomogeneous and given by the value of the exact solution on
\partial\Omega
. The following results are carried out with the choice of the nonlinearity example given by (
6.5
) and on a uniformly refined rectangular mesh of 10800 elements.

. We can observe that the estimators identify perfectly the error in the zone around the singularity at (0,0).

C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}/c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}
on our estimators. The left part of Figure
28
shows the relative exact errors
c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}^{1/2}\|\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\|{/\|\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\|}
together with their relative a posteriori error estimates
\eta^{k,i}{/\|\bm{u}_{h}^{k,i}\|}
from Theorem
6.7
.
The right part of Figure
28
displays the corresponding effectivity indices, given by the ratio of the estimate over the error (
6.34
). We see that the effectivity indices are not affected by the values of the
ratio
C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}/c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}
, which numerically gives the robustness of the estimate.

Figure 29:
[Section
6.8.2
], Standard linearization vs. adaptive linearization

In order to evaluate the potential of a stopping criterion for the linearization algorithm, we illustrate in the left part of Figure
29
the evolution of the total estimator
\eta^{k,i}
, the linearization estimator
\eta_{\rm lin}^{k,i}
, and the relative linearization residual

e_{\rm lin}^{k}
as a function of the number of Newton iterations for a uniform fixed mesh of
10800
elements and with the ratio
C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}/c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}=10
. These results are given with a standard resolution stopped when
e_{\rm lin}^{k}\leq 10^{-8}
. The linearization estimator and the relative linearization residual decrease as expected while the total estimator start to stagnate from the fifth iteration. Considering the stopping criterion (
6.35
) with
\Gamma_{\rm lin}=0.1
suggests that the resolution process can be halted after the seventh iteration, as it already provides a sufficiently accurate approximate solution while avoiding a significant number of unnecessary linearization iterations. In the right part of Figure
29
, computational savings in
terms of linearization iterations is observed for different values of the ratio
C_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}/c_{\tilde{\bm{\underline{K}}}}
. These results make it
apparent that using criteria based on balancing the error components gives a powerful tool for adaptivity algorithms. This is shown in the next section by considering Algorithm
7.1
applied to a realistic problem and achieving a significant gain in terms of the computation effort.
