7
Unsteady nonlinear coupled degenerate advection–diffusion–reaction problems. Temporal discretization error

In this section, the methodology derived in Sections
4
–
6
is applied to polytopal discretizations of a model real-life
multiphase compositional Darcy flow
in porous media. In the following, we describe briefly the multiphase model,
discuss its discretization by an implicit finite volume scheme, the
linearization by the Newton or combined Newton and fixed point method, and the algebraic solution of the arising
linear system. We explain that the required fictitious flux and potential reconstructions
stay unchanged with respect to Section
6
and finally develop our a posteriori error estimate with inexpensive implementation and evaluation distinguishing the different error components.

By decomposing the estimators into the space, time, linearization, and algebraic error components, we can design an algorithm involving adaptivity of both solvers of meshes. We namely formulate criteria for stopping the iterative algebraic solver and the iterative
linearization solver when the corresponding error components do not affect
significantly the total error. Moreover, the spatial and temporal error
components are balanced respectively by time step and space mesh adaptation, materializing the abstract considerations of Section
2.6
.
Denote by
\eta_{{\rm sp}}^{n,k,i},\eta_{{\rm tm}}^{n,k,i},\eta_{{\rm lin}}^{n,k,i}
, and
\eta_{{\rm alg}}^{n,k,i}

respectively the total spatial, temporal, linearization, and algebraic
estimators, on time step
n
, linearization step
k
, and algebraic solver
step
i
. Let
\gamma_{\rm lin},\gamma_{\rm alg}\in(0,1)
and
\Gamma_{\rm tm}>\gamma_{\rm tm}>0
be user-given fixed balancing
parameters and fix the fractions (percentages) of cells to refine

{\delta}_{\mathrm{ref}}
and to derefine
{\delta}_{\mathrm{deref}}
,
0<{\delta}_{\mathrm{deref}}<{\delta}_{\mathrm{ref}}<1
.
Following
[
124
,
253
,
70
,
103
,
104
]
and the references therein, we in particular derive in this
section the following
fully adaptive algorithm
:

Algorithm 7.1

(Adaptive stopping criteria and adaptive time and space mesh refinement)
.

Set
n:=0
.

while

t^{n}\leq t_{\rm{F}}

do

{
time loop
}

Set
n:=n+1
.

loop

{
spatial and temporal errors balancing loop
}

Set
k:=0
.

loop

{
linearization loop
}

Set
k:=k+1
.

Set up the linear system.

Set
i:=0
.

loop

{
algebraic solver loop
}

Perform a step of the iterative algebraic solver and set
i:=i+1
.

Evaluate the different a posteriori error estimators.

Terminate if:

\eta_{{\rm alg}}^{n,k,i}\leq\gamma_{\rm alg}\eta_{{\rm sp}}^{n,k,i}.

end

loop

{
algebraic solver loop
}

Terminate if:

\eta_{{\rm lin}}^{n,k,i}\leq\gamma_{\rm lin}\eta_{{\rm sp}}^{n,k,i}.

end

loop

{
linearization loop
}

Terminate if

\displaystyle{\eta_{{\rm sp}}^{{K},}{n{,k,i}}}

\displaystyle{\geq{\delta}_{\mathrm{ref}}\max_{{L}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}\big\{\eta_{{\rm sp}}^{{L},}{n{,k,i}}\big\}\qquad\forall{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}

\displaystyle\gamma_{\rm tm}\eta_{{\rm sp}}^{n,k,i}

\displaystyle\leq\eta_{{\rm tm}}^{n,k,i}\leq\Gamma_{\rm tm}\eta_{{\rm sp}}^{n,k,i};

else

Refine the cells
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}
such that
\eta_{{\rm sp}}^{{K},}{n{,k,i}}\geq{\delta}_{\mathrm{ref}}\max_{{L}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}\big\{\eta_{{\rm sp}}^{{L},}{n{,k,i}}\big\}
.

Derefine the cells
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}
such that
\eta_{{\rm sp}}^{{K},}{n{,k,i}}\leq{\delta}_{\mathrm{deref}}\max_{{L}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}\big\{\eta_{{\rm sp}}^{{L},}{n{,k,i}}\big\}
.

Refine the time step if
\eta_{{\rm tm}}^{n,k,i}>\Gamma_{\rm tm}\eta_{{\rm sp}}^{{\rm t}}{n,k,i}
, derefine the time step if
\gamma_{\rm tm}\eta_{{\rm sp}}^{n,k,i}>\eta_{{\rm tm}}^{n,k,i}
.

end

loop

{
spatial and temporal errors balancing loop
}

Update data.

end

while

{
time loop
}

7.1
Multiphase compositional unsteady Darcy flow

We consider the multiphase compositional Darcy flow model in porous media
[
4
,
28
,
93
,
83
,
261
]
.
We use the generalization of Coats’ formulation
[
94
]
to an arbitrary number of phases by
[
129
]
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\{p\}
be the set of
phases
,
\mathcal{C}=\{c\}
the set
of
components
, and, for a given phase
p\in\mathcal{P}
, let
\mathcal{C}_{p}\subset\mathcal{C}

be the set of its components.
S_{p}
then denotes the
saturation
of the
phase
p
and
C_{p,c}
the
molar fraction
of the component
c
in the
phase
p
. For a given component
c\in\mathcal{C}
, denote by
\mathcal{P}_{c}
the set of
the phases which contain
c
.

We denote by
P
the
reference pressure
such that the
phase
pressures

P_{p}
,
p\in\mathcal{P}
, are expressed as

P_{p}:=P+P_{\mathrm{c}_{p}}(S_{p}),

(7.1)

where
P_{\mathrm{c}_{p}}:{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{R}}
is a given generalized capillary pressure function.
We collect the unknowns of the model in the vector

{\bm{X}}{:=}(P,(S_{p})_{p\in\mathcal{P}},(C_{p,c})_{p\in\mathcal{P},c\in\mathcal{C}_{p}}).

(7.2)

For a phase
p\in\mathcal{P}
, let
k_{{\rm r},p}:{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{R}}
be the relative permeability function of the unknown saturation
S_{p}
. We denote by
\phi
the porosity of the medium, by
{\bm{\underline{K}}}
the permeability tensor, by
\mu_{p}
the dynamic viscosity, by
{\varsigma}_{p}
the molar density, and by
\rho_{p}
the mass density.
For simplicity, we suppose that all these porous media parameters are given as constants on each mesh element and on each time step;
\phi
is actually supposed to be constant in time. We also define the mobility by
\nu_{p}:={\varsigma}_{p}\frac{k_{{\rm r},p}}{\mu_{p}}
. Finally, let
q_{c}
be the
source or sink
term, again piecewise constant on the space-time mesh.

Let
t_{\mathrm{F}}>0
be the final simulation time.
The system of governing equations is posed in
\Omega\times(0,t_{\mathrm{F}})
by

\boxed{\partial_{t}{l}_{c}+{\nabla{\cdot}}\bm{\theta}_{c}=q_{c},\qquad\forall c\in\mathcal{C},}

(7.3)

where
{l}_{c}
is the
amount
(in moles) of component
c
per unit volume,

{l}_{c}:=\phi\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{c}}{\varsigma}_{p}S_{p}C_{p,c}.

(7.4)

In (
7.3
), for each component
c\in\mathcal{C}
, the
component flux

\bm{\theta}_{c}
has the following expression:

\bm{\theta}_{c}:=\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{c}}\bm{\theta}_{p,c},\qquad\bm{\theta}_{p,c}:=\bm{\theta}_{p,c}({\bm{X}})=\nu_{p}C_{p,c}\bm{v}_{p}({P_{p}}),

(7.5)

where for all phases
p\in\mathcal{P}
,
\bm{v}_{p}({P_{p}})
represents the average
phase
velocity
given by Darcy’s law,

\bm{v}_{p}({P_{p}}):=-{\bm{\underline{K}}}\left({\nabla}P_{p}+\rho_{p}g{\nabla}z\right).

(7.6)

Here,
z
is the vertical space coordinate and
g
is the gravitation acceleration constant. Note at this
occasion that only the first part of the Darcy velocity,

\bm{u}_{p}({P_{p}}):=-{\bm{\underline{K}}}{\nabla}P_{p},

(7.7)

is a gradient of the phase pressure. We will use this observation in our
error measure below. We assume that no-flow boundary conditions are
prescribed for all the component fluxes,

{\bm{\theta}_{c}}{\cdot}\bm{n}_{\Omega}=0\qquad\text{on $\partial\Omega\times(0,t_{\mathrm{F}})$}\qquad\forall c\in\mathcal{C},

(7.8)

where
\partial\Omega
denotes the boundary of
\Omega
and
\bm{n}_{\Omega}

its unit outward normal.
At
t=0
, we prescribe the
initial amount
of each
component,

{l}_{c}(\cdot,0)={l}_{c}^{0}\qquad\forall c\in\mathcal{C}.

(7.9)

The previous PDEs is supplemented by a system of algebraic equations imposing
the volume conservation

\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}}S_{p}=1,

(7.10)

the conservation of the quantity of matter

\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{p}}C_{p,c}=1\qquad\forall p\in\mathcal{P},

(7.11)

and local
thermodynamic equilibrium expressed by

\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}}(|\mathcal{P}_{c}|-1)=\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}}|\mathcal{C}_{p}|-|\mathcal{C}|

(7.12)

equalities of fugacities, where we refer to
[
103
]
for details.

7.2
Space-time mesh

We suppose that the time simulation interval
[0,t_{\mathrm{F}}]
is partitioned into time intervals
I^{n}:=[t^{n-1},t^{n}]
,
1\leq n\leq N
, given by the discrete times
0=t^{0}<\ldots<t^{n}<\ldots<t^{N}=t_{\mathrm{F}}
. The discrete time steps are then
\tau^{n}:=t^{n}-t^{n-1}
. Then, we let
(\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n})_{0\leq n\leq N}
be a family of polytopal meshes of the space domain
\Omega
defined in the sense of Section
3.6
; we suppose that

\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}
has been obtained from
\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n-1}
by refinement of some elements and
by coarsening of some other. For all
0\leq n\leq N
, we define
{\mathcal{F}}^{n}_{H}
,

\mathcal{F}_{h}^{n}
,
{\mathcal{F}}^{n}_{H,h}
,
\mathcal{F}_{{K},h}^{n,{\rm ext}}
, and
\mathcal{F}_{{K},h}^{n,{\rm int}}
as in Section
3.6
.

7.3
Weak solution and its properties

At this stage, we need to characterize a weak solution for the multiphase compositional
model (
7.1
)–(
7.12
).
We define

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle:=L^{2}((0,t_{\mathrm{F}});H^{1}(\Omega)),

(7.13a)

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle:=H^{1}((0,t_{\mathrm{F}});L^{2}(\Omega)).

(7.13b)

We equip the space
X
with the norm

\|\varphi\|_{X}:=\Biggl\{\sum_{n=1}^{N}\|\varphi\|_{X^{n}}^{2}\Biggr\}^{\frac{1}{2}},\qquad\|\varphi\|_{X^{n}}^{2}:=\int_{I^{n}}\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}\big\{h_{K}^{-2}\|\varphi\|_{{K}}^{2}+\|{\bm{\underline{K}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}{\nabla}\varphi\|_{{K}}^{2}\big\}\,\mathrm{d}t\qquad\varphi\in X,

(7.14)

where, we recall,
h_{K}
is the diameter of the cell
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}
. Below, it will be convenient to use
X^{n}:=L^{2}(I^{n};H^{1}(\Omega))
.
We suppose that there exists a unique weak solution of (
7.1
)–(
7.12
) in the following sense:

Assumption 7.2

(Weak solution)
.

There exists a weak solution
{\bm{X}}
of (
7.1
)–(
7.12
) which
can be characterized as follows:

\displaystyle l_{c}\in Y\qquad\forall c\in\mathcal{C},

(7.15a)

\displaystyle P_{p}\in X\qquad\forall p\in\mathcal{P},

(7.15b)

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}_{c}\in[L^{2}((0,t_{\mathrm{F}});L^{2}(\Omega))]^{d}\qquad\forall c\in\mathcal{C},

(7.15c)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{\mathrm{F}}}\left\{(\partial_{t}l_{c},\varphi)(t)-(\bm{\theta}_{c},{\nabla}\varphi)(t)\right\}\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{t_{\mathrm{F}}}(q_{c},\varphi)(t)\mathrm{d}t\qquad\forall\varphi\in X,\,\forall c\in\mathcal{C},

(7.15d)

\displaystyle\text{the initial condition~\eqref{eq:init} holds},

(7.15e)

\displaystyle\text{and the algebraic closure equations~\eqref{compositA}--\eqref{eq:fug} hold},

(7.15f)

where
P_{p}
,
l_{c}
, and
\bm{\theta}_{c}
are defined, respectively,
by (
7.1
), (
7.4
), and (
7.5
).

We have formulated the existence and uniqueness of a weak solution as an assumption, since, in contrast to the model problems of Sections
4
–
6
, the problem (
7.1
)–(
7.12
) is too abstract and complicated to prove it.
In simplified settings, with possibly only two phases present and each
phase composed of a single component, such results can be found
in
[
169
,
78
,
23
,
81
,
69
,
161
,
19
]
and the references therein.

Remark 7.3

(Component fluxes)
.

Since we assume
q_{c}
piecewise constant and thus
q_{c}\in L^{2}((0,t_{\mathrm{F}});

L^{2}(\Omega))
, it follows from (
7.15
) that actually

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}_{c}

\displaystyle\in L^{2}((0,t_{\mathrm{F}});{\mathbf{H}}(\mathrm{div},\Omega))\qquad

\displaystyle\forall c\in\mathcal{C},

(7.16a)

\displaystyle{\nabla{\cdot}}\bm{\theta}_{c}

\displaystyle=q_{c}-\partial_{t}l_{c}

\displaystyle\forall c\in\mathcal{C},

(7.16b)

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}_{c}{\cdot}\bm{n}_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=0\qquad\text{on $\partial\Omega\times(0,t_{\mathrm{F}})$}

\displaystyle\forall c\in\mathcal{C},

(7.16c)

so that the component fluxes
\bm{\theta}_{c}
have the normal trace
continuous in a proper sense, the governing equation (
7.3
)
is satisfied with a weak divergence, and the boundary
conditions (
7.8
) hold in the normal trace sense. This extends Proposition
4.1
, (
5.4
), and (
6.9
) to the multiphase compositional model (
7.1
)–(
7.12
).

7.4
Total error and its spatial discretization, temporal discretization, linearization, and algebraic components

Suppose that Assumption
7.2
holds, so that we dispose of a weak solution. Then, we describe the distance of the current approximate solution to the exact solution by the following intrinsic error measure:

\mathcal{N}^{n,k,i}:=\Bigg\{\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\big(\mathcal{N}^{n,k,i}_{c}\big)^{2}\Bigg\}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\Bigg\{\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}}\big(\mathcal{N}^{n,k,i}_{p}\big)^{2}\Bigg\}^{\frac{1}{2}},

(7.17a)

where

\mathcal{N}^{n,k,i}_{c}:=\sup_{\varphi\in X^{n},\|\varphi\|_{X^{n}}=1}\int_{I^{n}}\big\{(q_{c},\varphi)-(\partial_{t}{l}^{n,k,i}_{c,h\tau},\varphi)+\big(\bm{\theta}_{c,h\tau}^{n,k,i},{\nabla}\varphi\big)\big\}\,\mathrm{d}t

(7.17b)

is the
dual norm of the residual
of the weak formulation for each component flux
\bm{\theta}_{c,h\tau}^{n,k,i}
on the time interval
I^{n}
and where

\mathcal{N}^{n,k,i}_{p}:=\inf_{{\varsigma}_{p}\in X^{n}}\Bigg\{\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{p}}\int_{I^{n}}\Bigg\{{\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}}\big(\nu_{p,{K}}^{n,k,i}C^{n,k,i}_{p,c,{K}}\big)^{2}\|\bm{u}_{p,h\tau}^{n,k,i}+{\bm{\underline{K}}}\nabla{\varsigma}_{p}\|_{{\bm{\underline{K}}}^{-\frac{1}{2}},{K}}^{2}\Bigg\}\,\mathrm{d}t\Bigg\}^{\frac{1}{2}}

(7.17c)

evaluates the
nonconformity
(the distance of
\bm{u}_{p,h\tau}^{n,k,i}
to
{\bm{\underline{K}}}\nabla H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
) for each Darcy phase flux
\bm{u}_{p,h\tau}^{n,k,i}
.
Indeed, from (
7.16b
), the first term should be zero and from (
7.7
),
\bm{u}_{p}({P_{p}})
should be minus a
gradient of a scalar-valued function, which is not necessarily the case for
the discrete
\bm{u}_{p,h\tau}^{n,k,i}
given at time
t^{n}
.

Remark 7.4

(Error measure
\mathcal{N}^{n,k,i}
)
.

The error measure
\mathcal{N}^{n,k,i}
is a
natural extension of the energy error from the simpler cases. Indeed, component by component and phase by phase, it extends Theorem
4.9
from the Poisson case as well as Remark
6.6
from the steady nonlinear case. The sum
\sum_{n=1}^{N}(\mathcal{N}^{n,k,i})^{2}
(if the initial condition is satisfied exactly) then corresponds to the square of the energy error for the heat equation, see
[
120
, Theorem 2.1 and equation (2.7)]
.

In (
7.17
), we have denoted the approximate component fluxes by
\bm{\theta}_{c,h\tau}^{n,k,i}
and the approximate Darcy phase fluxes by
\bm{u}_{p,h\tau}^{n,k,i}
, anticipating that our numerical approximation will here consist in
temporal discretization
(with characteristic time step
\tau
and discrete times
t^{n}
),
spatial discretization
(with characteristic mesh size
h
),
iterative linearization
(with iteration index
k
), and
iterative algebraic resolution
(with iteration index
i
). Our goal will be to
distinguish the error components
as in (
2.14
) in Section
2.4.6
, to design an a posteriori error estimate of the form (
2.23b
), and to use them in the fully adaptive Algorithm
7.1
.

7.5
Backward Euler time discretization and locally conservative space discretization on polytopal meshes

To discretize problem (
7.1
)–(
7.12
), we consider a fully implicit backward Euler time stepping and an abstract locally conservative space discretization on a polytopal mesh of the form of Assumption
5.1
, using phase-upwinding.
For all
1\leq n\leq N
, we let
{\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n}:=({\bm{X}}^{n}_{{K}})_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}
, with, following (
7.2
),

{\bm{X}}^{n}_{{K}}:=(P^{n}_{{K}},(S^{n}_{p,{K}})_{p\in\mathcal{P}},(C^{n}_{p,c,{K}})_{p\in\mathcal{P},c\in\mathcal{C}_{p}})

(7.18)

be the algebraic vector of discrete unknowns on an element
{K}
.
System (
7.1
)–(
7.12
) is then discretized as follows: for all time
steps
1\leq n\leq N
, all polytopal cells
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}
, and each
component
c\in\mathcal{C}
, we require

\boxed{\frac{|{K}|}{\tau^{n}}\big({l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n}_{{K}})-{l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n-1}_{{K}})\big)+\sum_{{\sigma}\in{\mathcal{F}_{{K}}\cap\mathcal{F}_{H}^{\rm int}}}\theta_{c,{K},\sigma,\uparrow}({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n})=|{K}|q_{c,{K}}^{n}.}

(7.19)

This equation expresses the mass balance for the element
{K}
. Here

q_{c,{K}}^{n}
is the value of the source or sink
q_{c}
on the element
{K}
and time interval
I^{n}
– recall that we consider
q
piecewise constant. Recall also that
|{K}|
stands for the volume of the element
{K}
.

In a discrete version of (
7.4
), we set

{l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n}_{{K}}):=\phi_{K}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{c}}{\varsigma}^{n}_{p,{K}}S^{n}_{p,{K}}C^{n}_{p,c,{K}}.

(7.20)

Similarly, for each component
c\in\mathcal{C}
, following (
7.5
),
the total flux across the face
{\sigma}
results from the sum of the
corresponding fluxes for each phase
p\in\mathcal{P}_{c}
, i.e., for all elements
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}

and all faces
{\sigma}\in{\mathcal{F}_{{K}}\cap\mathcal{F}_{H}^{\rm int}}
with
{\sigma}{\subset}\partial{K}\cap\partial{L}
,

\theta_{c,{K},\sigma,\uparrow}({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n}):=\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{c}}\nu_{p,{K}^{\uparrow}_{p}}^{n}C^{n}_{p,c,{K}^{\uparrow}_{p}}v_{p,{K},\sigma}({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n}),\qquad{K}^{\uparrow}_{p}:=\begin{cases}{K}&\text{if $v_{p,{K},\sigma}({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n})\geq 0$},\\
{L}&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(7.21)

with
C_{p,c,{K}^{\uparrow}_{p}}^{n}
and
\nu_{p,{K}^{\uparrow}_{p}}^{n}
denoting,
respectively, the upstream molar fraction and the upstream mobility.
Let us also introduce

\theta_{c,{K},\sigma}({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n}):=\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{c}}\nu_{p,{K}}^{n}C^{n}_{p,c,{K}}v_{p,{K},\sigma}({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n}),

(7.22)

where no upwinding is used.
In (
7.21
)–(
7.22
), we have employed a finite volume approximation of the normal component of the average phase velocity on face

{\sigma}
given by

v_{p,{K},\sigma}({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n}):=v_{p,{K},\sigma}(\{{\bm{X}}^{n}_{{K}^{\prime}}\}_{{K}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{\sigma}}):=\sum_{{K}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{\sigma}}\tau_{{K}^{\prime}}^{\sigma}(P^{n}_{p,{K}^{\prime}}+\rho_{p,{\sigma}}^{n}gz_{{K}^{\prime}}),

(7.23)

where, following (
7.1
),

P^{n}_{p,{K}}:=P_{K}^{n}+P_{\mathrm{c}_{p}}(S_{p,{K}}^{n}),

(7.24)

and, for all
{\sigma}\in{\mathcal{F}_{{K}}\cap\mathcal{F}_{H}^{\rm int}}
,
\mathcal{S}_{\sigma}
is the
flux stencil collecting the elements in
\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}
with nonzero flux contribution (
\mathcal{S}_{\sigma}=\{{K},{L}\}
for
{\sigma}
such that
{\sigma}={\sigma}_{{K},{L}}=\partial{K}\cap\partial{L}\in\mathcal{F}_{H}^{\rm int}
) in the two-point scheme (
4.19b
). Moreover, for all
{L}\in\mathcal{S}_{\sigma}
,
\tau_{{L}}^{\sigma}\in{\mathbb{R}}
is the
transmissibility coefficient of the face
{\sigma}
(if
{\bm{\underline{K}}}={\bm{\underline{I}}}
,
\tau_{{K}}^{\sigma}=\frac{|{\sigma}_{{K},{L}}|}{d_{{K},{L}}}
and
\tau_{{L}}^{\sigma}=-\frac{|{\sigma}_{{K},{L}}|}{d_{{K},{L}}}
for the two-point scheme (
4.19b
)).
Please remark that the boundary fluxes are set to zero to account for the homogeneous
no-flow boundary conditions (
7.8
).

Finally, the initial condition comes from (
7.9
), and the algebraic constraints (
7.10
)–(
7.12
) are trivially discretized separately on all
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}
, namely

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}}S^{n}_{p,{K}}

\displaystyle=1,

(7.25)

\displaystyle\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{p}}C^{n}_{p,c,{K}}

\displaystyle=1\qquad\forall p\in\mathcal{P}.

(7.26)

7.6
Iterative linearization

At this stage, we need to solve, at each time step, the system of nonlinear
algebraic equations resulting from the discretization (
7.19
)–(
7.26
).
To this purpose, for all times
1\leq n\leq N
, we apply an iterative linearization, in extension of (
6.12
). We consider the
Newton
linearization
, as in (
6.13
). This generates, for an initial guess
{\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n,0}
(typically given by the previous time step/initial condition), a sequence

({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n,k})_{k\geq 1}
with
{\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n,k}
solution to the following
system of linear algebraic equations
: for all components
c\in\mathcal{C}
and all
mesh elements
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}
,

\sum_{{K}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}\frac{\partial{R}_{c,{K}}}{\partial{\bm{X}}^{n}_{{K}^{\prime}}}\big({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n,k-1}\big)\cdot\big({\bm{X}}^{n,k}_{{K}^{\prime}}-{\bm{X}}^{n,k-1}_{{K}^{\prime}}\big)+{R}_{c,{K}}\big({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n,k-1}\big)=0,

(7.27)

with, for all
c\in\mathcal{C}
and all
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}
,

{R}_{c,{K}}\big({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n}\big):=\frac{|{K}|}{\tau^{n}}{\big({l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n}_{{K}})-{l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n-1}_{{K}})\big)}+\sum_{{\sigma}\in{\mathcal{F}_{{K}}\cap\mathcal{F}_{H}^{\rm int}}}\theta_{c,{K},\sigma,\uparrow}\big({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n}\big)-|{K}|q_{c,{K}}^{n}.

Developing the derivatives in (
7.27
), we can write

\boxed{\frac{|{K}|}{\tau^{n}}\left({l}_{c,{K}}^{n,k}-{l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n-1}_{{K}})\right)+\sum_{{\sigma}\in{\mathcal{F}_{{K}}\cap\mathcal{F}_{H}^{\rm int}}}\theta_{c,{K},\sigma,\uparrow}^{n,k}-|{K}|q_{c,{K}}^{n}=0,}

(7.28)

where the linearization of the amount of component
{l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n,k}_{{K}})
of (
7.20
) is given by

{l}_{c,{K}}^{n,k}:={l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n,k-1}_{{K}})+\frac{\partial{l}_{c,{K}}}{\partial{\bm{X}}^{n}_{{K}}}\big({\bm{X}}^{n,k-1}_{{K}})\cdot\big({\bm{X}}^{n,k}_{{K}}-{\bm{X}}^{n,k-1}_{{K}}\big),

and where
\theta_{c,{K},\sigma,\uparrow}^{n,k}
are appropriate linearizations of the component fluxes
\theta_{c,{K},\sigma,\uparrow}({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n,k})
of (
7.21
).

Remark 7.5

(Blending Newton and fixed-point linearizations)
.

In numerical experiments below in Section
7.11
, we will actually define
\theta_{c,{K},\sigma,\uparrow}^{n,k}
by evaluation of the mobility
\nu_{p,{K}^{\uparrow}_{p}}^{n}
and the molar fraction
C^{n}_{p,c,{K}^{\uparrow}_{p}}
at the previous linearization iterate
k-1
as

\displaystyle\theta_{c,{K},\sigma,\uparrow}^{n,k}:=\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{c}}\biggl\{\nu_{p,{K}^{\uparrow}_{p}}^{n{,k-1}}C^{n{,k-1}}_{p,c,{K}^{\uparrow}_{p}}\biggl[v_{p,{K},\sigma}({{\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n,k-1}})+\sum_{{K}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}\frac{\partial v_{p,{K},\sigma}}{\partial{\bm{X}}^{n}_{{K}^{\prime}}}\big({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n,k-1}\big)\cdot\big({\bm{X}}^{n,k}_{{K}^{\prime}}-{\bm{X}}^{n,k-1}_{{K}^{\prime}}\big)\biggr]\biggr\},

blending thus the Newton and the fixed-point linearizations.

7.7
Iterative algebraic system solution

Now, for a given time step
1\leq n\leq N
and a given linearization iteration
k\geq 1
, (
7.28
) is a system of linear algebraic equations. To find its approximate solution, we use an
iterative algebraic solver
. Let
{\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n,k,0}
be an initial guess. Typically, we take
{\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n,k,0}={\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n,k-1}
. This gives a sequence

({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n,k,i})_{i\geq 1}
solving (
7.28
) up to the residuals
{R}^{n,k,i}_{c,{K}}
for all
c\in\mathcal{C}
and all
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}
, i.e.,

\boxed{\frac{|{K}|}{\tau^{n}}\left({l}_{c,{K}}^{n,k,i}-{l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n-1}_{{K}})\right)+\sum_{{\sigma}\in{\mathcal{F}_{{K}}\cap\mathcal{F}_{H}^{\rm int}}}\theta_{c,{K},\sigma,\uparrow}^{n,k,i}-|{K}|q_{c,{K}}^{n}=:{R}^{n,k,i}_{c,{K}},}

(7.29)

cf. (
6.14
)–(
6.15
). Here

{l}_{c,{K}}^{n,k,i}:={l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n,k-1}_{{K}})+\frac{\partial{l}_{c,{K}}}{\partial{\bm{X}}^{n}_{{K}}}\big({\bm{X}}^{n,k-1}_{{K}})\cdot\big({\bm{X}}^{n,k,i}_{{K}}-{\bm{X}}^{n,k-1}_{{K}}\big),

(7.30)

and, in the case of Remark
7.5
,

\displaystyle\theta_{c,{K},\sigma,\uparrow}^{n,k,i}:=\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{c}}\biggl\{\nu_{p,{K}^{\uparrow}_{p}}^{n{,k-1}}C^{n{,k-1}}_{p,c,{K}^{\uparrow}_{p}}\biggl[v_{p,{K},\sigma}({{\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n,k-1}})+\sum_{{K}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}\frac{\partial v_{p,{K},\sigma}}{\partial{\bm{X}}^{n}_{{K}^{\prime}}}\big({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n,k-1}\big)\cdot\big({\bm{X}}^{n,k,i}_{{K}^{\prime}}-{\bm{X}}^{n,k-1}_{{K}^{\prime}}\big)\biggr]\biggr\}.

(7.31)

We will also consider
j\geq 1
additional algebraic solver steps
in (
7.29
), similarly as we have done it
in (
6.16
).

7.8
Face normal fluxes and potential point values

We proceed here as in Section
6.5
. In particular, in extension of (
6.19
), we rely on the finite volume
face normal fluxes
. These employ the approximate phase normal velocities (
7.23
), the total normal flux nonlinear expressions (
7.22
) and their upwindings (
7.21
), as well as the linearizations (
7.31
) of the latter.
For all phases
p\in\mathcal{P}
, components
c\in\mathcal{C}
, time levels
n\geq 1
, mesh elements
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}
and their faces not lying on the boundary of
\Omega
,
{\sigma}\in{\mathcal{F}_{{K}}\cap\mathcal{F}_{H}^{\rm int}}
, linearization steps
k\geq 1
, and algebraic iterations
i\geq 1
, at times
t\in I^{n}
where necessary, we define

\displaystyle(\mathsf{U}_{{K},p}^{t,n,k,i})_{\sigma}

\displaystyle:={\frac{t-t^{n-1}}{\tau^{n}}}\sum_{{K}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{\sigma}}\tau_{{K}^{\prime}}^{\sigma}P^{n,k,i}_{p,{K}^{\prime}}{+\frac{t^{n}-t}{\tau^{n}}\sum_{{K}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{\sigma}}\tau_{{K}^{\prime}}^{\sigma}P^{n-1}_{p,{K}^{\prime}}},

(7.32a)

\displaystyle(\mathsf{\Theta}_{{\rm tm},{K},c}^{t,n,k,i})_{\sigma}

\displaystyle:={\frac{t^{n}-t}{\tau^{n}}}\big(\theta_{c,{K},\sigma}({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n,k,i})-\theta_{c,{K},\sigma}({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n-1})\big),

(7.32b)

\displaystyle(\mathsf{\Theta}_{{\rm{upw}},{K},c}^{n,k,i})_{\sigma}

\displaystyle:=\theta_{c,{K},\sigma,\uparrow}({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n,k,i})-\theta_{c,{K},\sigma}({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n,k,i}),

(7.32c)

\displaystyle(\mathsf{\Theta}_{{\rm lin},{K},c}^{n,k,i})_{\sigma}

\displaystyle:=\theta_{c,{K},\sigma,\uparrow}^{n,k,i}-\theta_{c,{K},\sigma,\uparrow}({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n,k,i}),

(7.32d)

\displaystyle(\mathsf{\Theta}_{{\rm alg},{K},c}^{n,k,i})_{\sigma}

\displaystyle:=\theta_{c,{K},\sigma,\uparrow}^{n,k,i+j}-\theta_{c,{K},\sigma,\uparrow}^{n,k,i}.

(7.32e)

All these face normal fluxes are set to zero on faces
{\sigma}
located on the boundary
of
\Omega
, in accordance with the no-flow boundary condition (
7.8
), with the exception of
(\mathsf{U}_{{K},p}^{t,n,k,i})_{\sigma}
that is set to
\langle{\bm{\underline{K}}}\rho_{p}g{\nabla}z{\cdot}\bm{n}_{\Omega},1\rangle_{\sigma}
, in accordance with (
7.6
)–(
7.7
). Note that (
7.32a
) is an affine-in-time interpolation of the previous time step
P^{n-1}_{p,{K}}
and the current
P^{n,k,i}_{p,{K}}
values.

We also need to define the phase pressure point values
\mathsf{Z}_{p,{K}}^{t,n,k,i}
and
\mathsf{Z}^{{\rm ext},{t,n,k,i}}_{p,{K}}
. We again proceed as in Section
6.5
, using directly the cells pressure values in Definition
5.11
or
5.13
. More
precisely, the starting values are the phase pressure values obtained from
the finite volume scheme, the current
P^{n,k,i}_{p,{K}}
and the previous
time step
P^{n-1}_{p,{K}}
, that we combine for each time
t\in I^{n}
to get an affine-in-time interpolation

\frac{t-t^{n-1}}{\tau^{n}}P^{n,k,i}_{p,{K}}+\frac{t^{n}-t}{\tau^{n}}P^{n-1}_{p,{K}}.

(7.33)

Note that all quantities of (
7.32
) and (
7.33
) are
readily available
from the
finite volume discretization
of
Section
7.5
, more precisely on each linearization step
k\geq 1

and each algebraic solver step
i\geq 1
as described in
Sections
7.6
–
7.7
.

7.9
Fictitious flux and potential reconstructions

We now identify the fictitious flux and potential reconstructions, following Section
6.6
.

Let a phase
p\in\mathcal{P}
, a component
c\in\mathcal{C}
, a time step
1\leq n\leq N
, a mesh element
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}
, a linearization iteration
k\geq 1
, and an algebraic solver iteration
i\geq 1

be fixed. For the fictitious flux reconstructions, we lift the face normal fluxes from (
7.32
), using them to define the spaces
\bm{V}_{h,\mathrm{N}}^{K}
in (
5.16
) of Definition
5.5
. We define:

•

The
Darcy phase fluxes reconstructions

\bm{u}_{p,h}^{n,k,i}|_{K}\in\bm{\mathcal{R\hskip-1.00006ptT}}\hskip-2.5pt_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{K})\cap\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},{K})
, as a discrete counterpart
of (
7.7
) at the discrete time
t^{n}
, using the face fluxes
\mathsf{U}_{{K},p}^{t,n,k,i}
of (
7.32a
) at
t^{n}
.

•

The
component fluxes reconstructions

\bm{\theta}_{c,h}^{n,k,i}|_{K}\in\bm{\mathcal{R\hskip-1.00006ptT}}\hskip-2.5pt_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{K})\cap\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},{K})
, as a discrete counterpart of (
7.5
) at the discrete time
t^{n}
, prescribed by
\theta_{c,{K},\sigma}({\bm{X}}_{\mathcal{T}_{H}}^{n,k,i})
appearing in (
7.32b
).

•

The
upwinding error component fluxes reconstructions

\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{{upw}},c,h}^{n,k,i}|_{K}\in\bm{\mathcal{R\hskip-1.00006ptT}}\hskip-2.5pt_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{K})\cap\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},{K})
prescribed by
\mathsf{\Theta}_{{\rm{upw}},{K},c}^{n,k,i}
of (
7.32c
).

•

The
linearization error flux reconstruction

\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin},c,h}^{n,k,i}|_{K}\in\bm{\mathcal{R\hskip-1.00006ptT}}\hskip-2.5pt_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{K})\cap\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},{K})
prescribed by
\mathsf{\Theta}_{{\rm lin},{K},c}^{n,k,i}
of (
7.32d
).

•

The
algebraic error flux reconstruction

\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{alg},c,h}^{n,k,i}|_{K}\in\bm{\mathcal{R\hskip-1.00006ptT}}\hskip-2.5pt_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{K})\cap\bm{H}(\mathrm{div},{K})
prescribed by
\mathsf{\Theta}_{{\rm alg},{K},c}^{n,k,i}
of (
7.32e
).

The error measure
\mathcal{N}^{n,k,i}
of (
7.17
) works with approximate Darcy phase fluxes
\bm{u}_{p,h\tau}^{n,k,i}
together with approximate component fluxes
\bm{\theta}_{c,h\tau}^{n,k,i}
.
We define them as affine-in-time on the time interval
I^{n}
, given respectively by
\bm{u}_{p,h}^{n,k,i}
and
\bm{\theta}_{c,h}^{n,k,i}
at the current time
t^{n}
, and by
\bm{u}_{p,h}^{n-1}
and
\bm{\theta}_{c,h}^{n-1}
obtained in the same way but on time previous time
t^{n-1}
, once the linearization and algebraic iterations have been stopped. All
\bm{u}_{p,h\tau}^{n,k,i}
,
\bm{\theta}_{c,h\tau}^{n,k,i}
, and the other fluxes
\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{{upw}},c,h}^{n,k,i}
,
\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin},c,h}^{n,k,i}
, and
\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{alg},c,h}^{n,k,i}
are only used for the proof of Theorem
7.10
below and
need not be constructed in practice
(whence, recall, the naming fictitious).
It namely follows from (
7.29
), on algebraic step
i+j
,
the above flux reconstructions, and the Green theorem that
there holds

q_{c,{K}}^{n}-\frac{{l}_{c,{K}}^{n,k,i+j}-{l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n-1}_{{K}})}{\tau^{n}}-{\big(}{\nabla{\cdot}}({\bm{\theta}_{c,h}^{n,k,i}+}\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{{upw}},c,h}^{n,k,i}+\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin},c,h}^{n,k,i}+\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{alg},c,h}^{n,k,i}){\big)|_{K}}=-|{K}|^{-1}{R}^{n,k,i+j}_{c,{K}}\quad\forall{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n},

(7.34)

similarly as in (
6.22
).

The fictitious reconstruction of the phase pressures is again achieved as in Definition
5.14
, i.e., setting

\zeta_{p,{h\tau}}^{n,k,i}({\bm{a}}):=\mathsf{Z}_{p,{\bm{a}}}^{t,n,k,i}\qquad\forall{\bm{a}}\in\mathcal{V}_{h}

from the potential point values identified in Section
7.8
. It is piecewise
affine with respect to the simplicial submeshes
\mathcal{T}_{h}^{n}
and
H^{1}(\Omega)
-conforming and piecewise affine in time. In the error measure
\mathcal{N}^{n,k,i}
of (
7.17
), we also need
{l}^{n,k,i}_{c,h\tau}
. It is defined as affine-in-time on each time interval
I^{n}
, given
respectively by the values
{l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n-1}_{{K}})
and
{l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n,k,i}_{{K}})
at times
t^{n-1}
and
t^{n}
, for all
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}
. This in particular gives

\partial_{t}{l}^{n,k,i}_{c,h\tau}|_{K}=\frac{{l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n,k,i}_{{K}})-{l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n-1}_{{K}})}{\tau^{n}}

(7.35)

on each time interval
I^{n}
.

7.10
A guaranteed a posteriori error estimate distinguishing the error
components with inexpensive implementation and evaluation

Proceeding as for the steady linear problem in Section
5.9
and
the steady nonlinear problem in Section
6.7
(under
Assumption
5.1
), we obtain a posteriori error estimates for the problem (
7.1
)–(
7.12
). We first treat the simpler case where there is no coarsening of the space meshes between the time steps and postpone the general case to Remark
7.8
below.

Theorem 7.6
(A guaranteed a posteriori error estimate distinguishing the error
components with inexpensive implementation and evaluation)
.

For the multiphase compositional Darcy flow (
7.1
)–(
7.12
), let the weak solution
{\bm{X}}
satisfy (
7.2
). Consider any polytopal discretization of the form (
7.19
), an iterative linearization (
7.27
) on step
k\geq 1
, and any
iterative algebraic solver (
7.29
) on step
i\geq 1
, for a given time step
1\leq n\leq N
.
Consider
j\geq 1
additional algebraic solver steps.
Let, for each polytopal element
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}
, the element vectors of the face normal fluxes
\mathsf{\Theta}_{\bullet,{K},c}^{{n,k,i}}
,
\bullet={\rm upw},{\rm lin},{\rm alg}
, together with
\mathsf{\Theta}_{{\rm tm},{K},c}^{t,n,k,i}
and
\mathsf{U}_{{K},p}^{t,n,k,i}
be given by (
7.32
).
Let the element vectors of potential point values
\mathsf{Z}_{p,{K}}^{t,n,k,i}
and
\mathsf{Z}^{{\rm ext},{t,n,k,i}}_{p,{K}}
be constructed from the time-interpolated values (
7.33
) of
P^{n,k,i}_{p,{K}}
and
P^{n-1}_{p,{K}}
following Section
7.8
. Consider the approximate solution
\bm{u}_{p,h\tau}^{n,k,i},\bm{\theta}_{c,h\tau}^{n,k,i}
as defined in Section
7.9
and the intrinsic error measure
\mathcal{N}^{n,k,i}
of (
7.17
).
Let finally the element matrices

\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{\mathrm{MFE},{K}}
,

\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{\mathrm{FE},{K}}
, and

\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{FE},{K}}
be respectively defined
by (
5.20
), (
5.32
),
and (
5.33
). Let
\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}\subset\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n-1}
, i.e.,
there is no coarsening. Then

\mathcal{N}^{n,k,i}\leq\Bigg\{\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\big(\eta_{{\rm sp},c}^{n,k,i}+\eta_{{\rm tm},c}^{n,k,i}+\eta_{{\rm lin},c}^{n,k,i}+\eta_{{\rm alg},c}^{n,k,i}+\eta_{{\rm rem},c}^{n,k,i}\big)^{2}\Bigg\}^{\frac{1}{2}}

(7.36)

with

\eta_{{\rm\bullet},c}^{n,k,i}:=\Bigg\{{\delta_{\bullet}}\int_{I^{n}}\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}\big(\eta_{{\rm\bullet},{K},c}^{n,k,i}\big)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\Bigg\}^{\frac{1}{2}},

(7.37)

where
\bullet={\rm sp},{\rm tm},{\rm lin},{\rm alg},{\rm rem}

and
\delta_{\bullet}:=2
, except for
\delta_{\rm sp}:=4
. Here, for

c\in\mathcal{C}
, we prescribe the elementwise
spatial estimators

\eta_{{\rm sp},{K},c}^{n,k,i}:=\eta_{{\rm{upw}},{K},c}^{n,k,i}+\Bigg\{\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{c}}\left(\eta_{{\rm NC},{K},c,p}^{t,n,k,i}\right)^{2}\Bigg\}^{\frac{1}{2}},

(7.38a)

with

\begin{split}\left(\eta_{{\rm NC},{K},c,p}^{t,n,k,i}\right)^{2}:={}&\left(\nu_{p,{K}}^{n,k,i}C^{n,k,i}_{p,c,{K}}\right)^{2}\biggl[\big(\mathsf{U}_{{K},p}^{t,n,k,i}\big)^{\mathrm{t}}\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{\mathrm{MFE},{K}}\mathsf{U}_{{K},p}^{t,n,k,i}+\big(\mathsf{Z}_{p,{K}}^{t,n,k,i}\big)^{\mathrm{t}}\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{\mathrm{FE},{K}}\mathsf{Z}_{p,{K}}^{t,n,k,i}\\
{}&+2\big(\mathsf{U}_{{K},p}^{t,n,k,i}\big)^{\mathrm{t}}\mathsf{Z}^{{\rm ext},{t,n,k,i}}_{p,{K}}-2{\sum_{{\sigma}\in\mathcal{F}_{{K}}}(\mathsf{U}_{{K},p}^{t,n,k,i})_{\sigma}|{K}|^{-1}}{\mathsf{1}}^{\mathrm{t}}\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{FE},{K}}\mathsf{Z}_{p,{K}}^{t,n,k,i}\biggr],\end{split}

(7.38b)

and the
upwinding estimators

\left(\eta_{{\rm{upw}},{K},c}^{n,k,i}\right)^{2}:=(\mathsf{\Theta}_{{\rm{upw}},{K},c}^{n,k,i})^{\mathrm{t}}\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{\mathrm{MFE},{K}}(\mathsf{\Theta}_{{\rm{upw}},{K},c}^{n,k,i})

(7.38c)

together with the
temporal estimators

\left(\eta_{{\rm tm},{K},c}^{n,k,i}\right)^{2}:=(\mathsf{\Theta}_{{\rm tm},{K},c}^{t,n,k,i})^{\mathrm{t}}\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{\mathrm{MFE},{K}}\mathsf{\Theta}_{{\rm tm},{K},c}^{t,n,k,i},

(7.38d)

the
linearization
estimators

\left(\eta_{{\rm lin},{K},c}^{n,k,i}\right)^{2}:=(\mathsf{\Theta}_{{\rm lin},{K},c}^{n,k,i})^{\mathrm{t}}\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{\mathrm{MFE},{K}}\mathsf{\Theta}_{{\rm lin},{K},c}^{n,k,i}+h_{K}(\tau^{n})^{-1}\|{l}_{c,{K}}^{n,k,i}-{l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n,k,i}_{{K}})\|_{{K}},

(7.38e)

the
algebraic estimators

\left(\eta_{{\rm alg},{K},c}^{n,k,i}\right)^{2}:=(\mathsf{\Theta}_{{\rm alg},{K},c}^{n,k,i})^{\mathrm{t}}\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{\mathrm{MFE},{K}}\mathsf{\Theta}_{{\rm alg},{K},c}^{n,k,i}+h_{K}(\tau^{n})^{-1}\|{l}_{c,{K}}^{n,k,i+j}-{l}_{c,{K}}^{n,k,i}\|_{{K}},

(7.38f)

and the
algebraic remainder estimators
by

\eta_{{\rm rem},{K},c}^{n,k,i}:=h_{K}|{K}|^{-1/2}|{R}^{n,k,i+j}_{c,{K}}|.

(7.38g)

Proof.

Let the time step
1\leq n\leq N
, the iterative linearization step
k\geq 1
, and the iterative algebraic solver
i\geq 1
be fixed. We bound the two building terms
\mathcal{N}^{n,k,i}_{c}
and
\mathcal{N}^{n,k,i}_{p}
of the error measure
\mathcal{N}^{n,k,i}
from (
7.17
) separately, for each component
c\in\mathcal{C}
and each phase
p\in\mathcal{P}
. We rely on the fictitious flux and potential reconstructions of Section
7.9
and namely on (
7.34
).

We first bound
\mathcal{N}^{n,k,i}_{c}
. Let
\varphi\in X^{n}
with
\|\varphi\|_{X^{n}}=1
be fixed. We have, using (
7.35
), (
7.34
), and the Green theorem

\displaystyle(q_{c},\varphi)-(\partial_{t}{l}^{n,k,i}_{c,h\tau},\varphi)+\big(\bm{\theta}_{c,h\tau}^{n,k,i},{\nabla}\varphi\big)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}\left(q_{c}-\frac{{l}_{c,{K}}^{n,k,i+j}-{l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n-1}_{{K}})}{\tau^{n}}-{\nabla{\cdot}}({\bm{\theta}_{c,h}^{n,k,i}+}\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{{upw}},c,h}^{n,k,i}+\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin},c,h}^{n,k,i}+\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{alg},c,h}^{n,k,i}),\varphi\right)_{K}

\displaystyle+\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}\left(\frac{{l}_{c,{K}}^{n,k,i+j}-{l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n-1}_{{K}})}{\tau^{n}}-\frac{{l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n,k,i}_{{K}})-{l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n-1}_{{K}})}{\tau^{n}},\varphi\right)_{K}

\displaystyle+({\nabla{\cdot}}({\bm{\theta}_{c,h}^{n,k,i}+}\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{{upw}},c,h}^{n,k,i}+\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin},c,h}^{n,k,i}+\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{alg},c,h}^{n,k,i}),\varphi)+\big(\bm{\theta}_{c,h\tau}^{n,k,i},{\nabla}\varphi\big)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}(|{K}|^{-1}{R}^{n,k,i+j}_{c,{K}},\varphi)_{K}+\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}\left(\frac{{l}_{c,{K}}^{n,k,i+j}-{l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n,k,i}_{{K}})}{\tau^{n}},\varphi\right)_{K}

\displaystyle+\big(\bm{\theta}_{c,h\tau}^{n,k,i}-{\bm{\theta}_{c,h}^{n,k,i}-}\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{{upw}},c,h}^{n,k,i}-\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin},c,h}^{n,k,i}-\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{alg},c,h}^{n,k,i},{\nabla}\varphi\big)

We now treat the terms herein separately. First, the triangle inequality gives

\displaystyle\big(\bm{\theta}_{c,h\tau}^{n,k,i}-{\bm{\theta}_{c,h}^{n,k,i}-}\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{{upw}},c,h}^{n,k,i}-\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin},c,h}^{n,k,i}-\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{alg},c,h}^{n,k,i},{\nabla}\varphi\big)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}\left\{\|\bm{\theta}_{c,h\tau}^{n,k,i}-\bm{\theta}_{c,h}^{n,k,i}\|_{{\bm{\underline{K}}}^{-\frac{1}{2}},{K}}+\|\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{{upw}},c,h}^{n,k,i}\|_{{\bm{\underline{K}}}^{-\frac{1}{2}},{K}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\|\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin},c,h}^{n,k,i}\|_{{\bm{\underline{K}}}^{-\frac{1}{2}},{K}}+\|\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{alg},c,h}^{n,k,i}\|_{{\bm{\underline{K}}}^{-\frac{1}{2}},{K}}\right\}\|{\bm{\underline{K}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}{\nabla}\varphi\|_{K}.

Second, the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality gives

\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}\left(\frac{{l}_{c,{K}}^{n,k,i+j}-{l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n,k,i}_{{K}})}{\tau^{n}},\varphi\right)_{K}\leq\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}h_{K}(\tau^{n})^{-1}\|{l}_{c,{K}}^{n,k,i+j}-{l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n,k,i}_{{K}})\|_{K}h_{K}^{-1}\|\varphi\|_{K}.

And third,

-\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}(|{K}|^{-1}{R}^{n,k,i+j}_{c,{K}},\varphi)_{K}\leq\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}h_{K}|{K}|^{-1/2}|{R}^{n,k,i+j}_{c,{K}}|h_{K}^{-1}\|\varphi\|_{K}.

Thus, regrouping these estimates and using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality together with (
7.14
), we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{N}^{n,k,i}_{c}\leq{}&\sup_{\varphi\in X^{n},\|\varphi\|_{X^{n}}=1}\int_{I^{n}}\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}\big(\eta_{{\rm tm},{K},c}^{n,k,i}+\eta_{{\rm{upw}},{K},c}^{n,k,i}+\eta_{{\rm lin},{K},c}^{{n,k,i}}+\eta_{{\rm alg},{K},c}^{{n,k,i}}+\eta_{{\rm rem},{K},c}^{n,k,i}\big)\\
{}&\big\{h_{K}^{-2}\|\varphi\|_{{K}}^{2}+\|{\bm{\underline{K}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}{\nabla}\varphi\|_{{K}}^{2}\big\}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
\leq{}&\sup_{\varphi\in X^{n},\|\varphi\|_{X^{n}}=1}\left\{\int_{I^{n}}\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}\big(\eta_{{\rm tm},{K},c}^{n,k,i}+\eta_{{\rm{upw}},{K},c}^{n,k,i}+\eta_{{\rm lin},{K},c}^{{n,k,i}}+\eta_{{\rm alg},{K},c}^{{n,k,i}}+\eta_{{\rm rem},{K},c}^{n,k,i}\big)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}}\underbrace{\|\varphi\|_{X^{n}}}_{=1},\end{split}

(7.39)

where, for all components
c\in\mathcal{C}
and all
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\eta_{{\rm tm},{K},c}^{n,k,i}

\displaystyle:=\|\bm{\theta}_{c,h\tau}^{n,k,i}-\bm{\theta}_{c,h}^{n,k,i}\|_{{\bm{\underline{K}}}^{-\frac{1}{2}},{K}},

(7.40a)

\displaystyle\eta_{{\rm{upw}},{K},c}^{n,k,i}

\displaystyle:=\|\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{{upw}},c,h}^{n,k,i}\|_{{\bm{\underline{K}}}^{-\frac{1}{2}},{K}},

(7.40b)

\displaystyle\eta_{{\rm lin},{K},c}^{{n,k,i}}

\displaystyle:=\|\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{lin},c,h}^{n,k,i}\|_{{\bm{\underline{K}}}^{-\frac{1}{2}},{K}}{+h_{K}(\tau^{n})^{-1}\|{l}_{c,{K}}^{n,k,i}-{l}_{c,{K}}({\bm{X}}^{n,k,i}_{{K}})\|_{K}},

(7.40c)

\displaystyle\eta_{{\rm alg},{K},c}^{{n,k,i}}

\displaystyle:=\|\bm{\theta}_{\mathrm{alg},c,h}^{n,k,i}\|_{{\bm{\underline{K}}}^{-\frac{1}{2}},{K}}+h_{K}(\tau^{n})^{-1}\|{l}_{c,{K}}^{n,k,i+j}-{l}_{c,{K}}^{n,k,i}\|_{K},

(7.40d)

\displaystyle\eta_{{\rm rem},{K},c}^{n,k,i}

\displaystyle:=h_{K}|{K}|^{-1/2}|{R}^{n,k,i+j}_{c,{K}}|.

(7.40e)

We now bound
\mathcal{N}^{n,k,i}_{p}
. Let

\displaystyle\eta_{{\rm NC},{K},c,p}^{t,n,k,i}

\displaystyle:=\nu_{p,{K}}^{n,k,i}C^{n,k,i}_{p,c,{K}}\|\bm{u}_{p,h\tau}^{n,k,i}+{\bm{\underline{K}}}\nabla{\zeta_{p,{h\tau}}^{n,k,i}}\|_{{\bm{\underline{K}}}^{-\frac{1}{2}},{K}}.

(7.40f)

We have

\mathcal{N}^{n,k,i}_{p}\leq\Bigg\{\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{p}}\int_{I^{n}}\Bigg\{{\sum_{{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}}}\big(\eta_{{\rm NC},{K},c,p}^{t,n,k,i}\big)^{2}\Bigg\}\,\mathrm{d}t\Bigg\}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(7.41)

We now remark that the estimators in (
7.40a
)–(
7.40f
) and (
7.38b
)–(
7.38g
) are indeed the same, proceeding as in Theorem
5.9
to evaluate the nonconformity estimator
given by (
7.40f
) and by applying Lemma
5.8
to
evaluate the upwinding, temporal, linearization, and algebraic estimators.
Regrouping the spatial discretization estimators

\displaystyle\eta_{{\rm sp},{K},c}^{n,k,i}

\displaystyle:=\eta_{{\rm{upw}},{K},c}^{n,k,i}+\Bigg\{\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{c}}\left(\eta_{{\rm NC},{K},c,p}^{t,n,k,i}\right)^{2}\Bigg\}^{\frac{1}{2}}

(7.42)

and all the estimators per component
c\in\mathcal{C}
, using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, and recalling (
7.17
), we obtain (
7.36
).∎

Remark 7.7

(Sharper estimates)
.

Sharper estimates, namely avoiding the factors
\delta_{\bullet}
, (but not identifying/regrouping the error component estimators) immediately follow from (
7.39
) and (
7.41
).

Remark 7.8

(Coarsening)
.

If a mesh element
{K}\in\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}
has
been obtained by coarsening of elements of the previous mesh
\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n-1}

collected in the set
\mathcal{T}^{n-1}_{H,{K}}
, then the estimators

\eta_{{\rm NC},{K},c,p}^{t,n,k,i}
of (
7.38b
) and

\eta_{{\rm tm},{K},c}^{n,k,i}
of (
7.38d
) need to be evaluated
using the matrices
\widehat{\mathbb{A}}_{\mathrm{MFE},{K}}
,

\widehat{\mathbb{S}}_{\mathrm{FE},{K}}
, and

\widehat{\mathbb{M}}_{\mathrm{FE},{K}}
stemming from a common refinement of
the element
{K}
by the simplicial meshes
\mathcal{T}_{h}^{n-1}|_{\mathcal{T}^{n-1}_{H,{K}}}

and
\mathcal{T}_{K}
and not
\mathcal{T}_{K}
solely. In this sense, Theorem
7.10

also holds when
\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n}\not\subset\mathcal{T}_{H}^{n-1}
.

7.11
Numerical experiments

The proposed a
posteriori error estimate framework summarized in Theorem
7.10
and
Algorithm
7.1

has been implemented in a reservoir
prototype simulator
[
223
]
, a thermal multi-purpose simulator written
in C++, which is a part of the next generation IFPEn research simulators
based on the
Arcane
framework
[
141
]
. The
execution platform is a public computer Intel Core i7, 8 cores, 3.7Ghz with
16GB of memory.

7.11.1
Setup

We study two different test cases. The first one
is taken from
[
89
]
, relying on the tenth SPE comparative
solution project model. It is an incompressible water-oil two-phase flow
problem built on a Cartesian regular geometry. This test corresponds to the
layer 85 of SPE10. We choose here first the Cartesian regular mesh so as to
compare our new approach with approaches already validated on this type of
meshes. The second case is a simulation of a black-oil model. For this test,
the problem is built on a three-dimensional corner-point geometry (distorted
grids), well-known and most often used in reservoir simulation due to the
flexibility allowing a good representation of reservoir description,
see
[
105
]
and the references therein.

In our computational experiments presented below, we perform a
nonconforming-type mesh refinement and allow one hanging node per cell face
for the space mesh adaptation. The resulting mesh is a polytopal mesh in the sense of Section
3.6
. Coarsening is then obtained by
retrogressive cell agglomeration. During the coarsening and refinement phases, we use some upscaling and interpolating mechanisms
to ensure the mass balance. Geological data (rock properties like porosity and
permeability) are stored at the finest mesh level as data on a cloud
of space points. These data are used during mesh adaptation by standard
upscaling algorithms to compute the values of the rock model properties on
the newly created cells following
[
185
]
.

7.11.2
Two-phase Darcy flow

We consider a two-dimensional spatial domain
discretized by a grid of
60\times 220
rectangular cells of size
6.096
m
in the
x
direction and
3.048
m in the
y
direction. We choose the
initial time step as
\tau^{0}=4.32\times 10^{4}
s, which equals to
0.5
days,
and the process is simulated to
t_{F}=2000
days. The reservoir is initially
saturated with hydrocarbons and we consider the injection of water by a well
located at the center of the grid. Four production wells are placed at the
four corners of the domain. Therefore, we have a water component
{\rm W}

and an oil component
{\rm O}
collected in the set of components
\mathcal{C}=\{\rm W,O\}
and two phases
\mathcal{P}=\{\rm w,o\}
corresponding to water and oil. The
model is actually simplified in that the components can be identified with
the phases, so that
\mathcal{P}_{\rm W}=\{{\rm w}\}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\rm O}=\{{\rm o}\}
,
C_{{\rm w},{\rm W}}=C_{{\rm o},{\rm O}}=1
,
C_{{\rm o},{\rm W}}=C_{{\rm w},{\rm O}}=0
, and the vector of unknowns
{\bm{X}}
reduces to

(P,(S_{p})_{p\in\mathcal{P}})
. Note that then the accumulation term
{l}_{c}

becomes linear in the only unknown
S_{p}
, and one particular consequence is
that the second term in the definition (
7.38e
) of

\eta_{{\rm lin},{K},c}^{n,k,i}
vanishes. The porosity
\phi
and the
permeability field
{\bm{\underline{K}}}
(scalar coefficient times an identity matrix) are
shown in Figure
30
. The other parameters of
Section
7.1
are given by (see
[
89
]
for
more details):

•

\mu_{\rm{o}}=10^{-3}
Pa
\cdot
s and
\mu_{\rm{w}}=0.3\cdot 10^{-3}
Pa
\cdot
s,

•

{\varsigma}_{\rm o}={\varsigma}_{\rm w}=1
mole
\cdot
m
-3
,

•

S_{\rm wr}=S_{\rm or}=0.2
,

•

k_{{{\rm r},{\rm w}}}(S_{{\rm w}})=\left(\frac{S_{{\rm w}}-S_{\rm wr}}{1-S_{\rm wr}-S_{\rm or}}\right)^{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad k_{{{\rm r},{\rm o}}}(S_{{\rm o}})=\left(\frac{S_{{\rm o}}-S_{\rm or}}{1-S_{\rm wr}-S_{\rm or}}\right)^{2},

•

P_{\mathrm{c}_{p}}(S_{p})=0
,

•

there is no gravitational force,
z=0
, so that the mass densities

\rho_{p}
need not be specified.

Figure 30:
[Section
7.11.2
] Porosity (
left
) and permeability (
right
), layer 85 of the 10th SPE case

We consider a discretization by the implicit multi-point finite volume scheme
of Section
7.5
with the linearization detailed in
Section
7.6
, using the blending of Newton and fixed-point of Remark
7.5
. For the linear solver needed in Section
7.7
, we use the Bi-Conjugated
Gradient Stabilized (BiCGStab)
[
239
]
with an ILU
\{0\}

preconditioner.

Figure
31
shows, at
500
days of the simulation, the
evolution of the approximate water saturation, the spatial estimator computed
by the formula on Cartesian grid proposed
in
[
262
, Chapter 4]
, and the spatial estimator given
by (
7.38a
). Similar behavior is observed for
the two a posteriori estimators. Furthermore, we see that they both detect
well the error following the saturation front despite the strong
heterogeneity of the domain.

days

The previous result motivates an adaptive mesh refinement/coarsening (AMR)
strategy based on these estimators. We now verify that the simple a
posteriori estimators on polyhedral mesh of Theorem
7.10

give the expected results, while comparing it with the already validated case
in
[
262
, Chapter 4]
. We focus on the mesh space
adaptivity and we do not act on the time step. Actually, in our simulator,
the time step size is controlled by the behavior of the Newton/fixed-point algorithm
(increased systematically in general, by multiplying by 2, and divided by 2
if the Newton/fixed-point algorithm diverges), so the space-mesh adaptation only will
enable an easy comparison with the non-adaptive code. We first apply
Algorithm
7.1
with

{\delta}_{\mathrm{ref}}=0.7,{\delta}_{\mathrm{deref}}=0.2
, and “exact”
(i.e., negligible-error) algebraic and linearization solvers. On the
coarse scale, the domain is discretized by a grid of
30\times 110
cells
and we allow one refinement level. Figure
32
shows the
evolution of the approximate water saturation and of the meshes at two
different simulation times. We remark that the refinement follows the
saturation front as times evolves. Additionally, since we have a
model with highly heterogeneous permeability, we are lead to perform a slow
derefinement process in the zone abandoned by the front of water saturation.
Figure
33
depicts the cumulated oil rate (left) and the
water-cut
2
2

2

The ratio of water produced compared to the volume of
total liquids produced.
(right) during the simulation. We compare there the
results on the fine grid, the results on the coarse grid, and the
results of the two AMR strategies. We remark that the accuracy of the results
on the fine grid is almost recovered by the AMR strategy, and appears much
better than the coarse-grid result.

The details of the efficiency of the AMR strategy based on the a posteriori
error estimator can finally be appreciated in Table
1
. We
compare the global CPU time of the simulation for the different strategies.
We detail the CPU time spent on the evaluation of the estimators and the mesh
adaptation. We remark a low cost of the estimators evaluation compared with
the total computation CPU time, thanks to the use of the simple and
fast-to-evaluate form of a posteriori error estimates on polygonal meshes of
Theorem
7.10
. In Table
1
, when applying
the mesh adaptation, the overall CPU time is the sum of the resolution
time, AMR time, and estimators evaluation time. We remark that applying the
AMR strategy on this two-dimensional test case leads to a gain factor in the
overall CPU time at around 2, for both the polygonal estimate proposed
in this paper and the Cartesian estimate already validated on this type of
meshes. Note that, however, that the simulation is slightly faster when
adapting using the Cartesian estimate, due to the fact that we can here
directly compute the
\bm{\mathcal{R\hskip-1.00006ptT}}\hskip-2.5pt
basis functions on the rectangular cells.

7.11.3
Three-phases, three-components Darcy flow

In this section, we present a simulation of a black-oil model.
Here, we have three phases constituted by water, oil, and gas, represented by
lowercase letters w, o, g as indices, respectively. The oil phase contains
two types of components: nonvolatile oil and volatile oil, which we call here
oil component and gas component, respectively. This is due to the fact that
in this model, the hydrocarbon components are divided into light and heavy
components. The light component can dissolve into the liquid oil phase or
volatilize in the gas phase according to the pressure and temperature. The
gas phase only contains the gas components and the water phase only
contains the water component. The components are represented by uppercase
letters W for the water component, O for the oil component, and G for the gas
component. Therefore, we have a problem with three phases
\mathcal{P}=\{\rm w,o,g\}

and three components
\mathcal{C}=\{\rm W,O,G\}
.

Figure 34:
[Section
7.11.3
] Permeability

Model setting

The reservoir considered in this test case is a
3
-dimensional domain
\Omega:=4750\text{m}\times 3000\text{m}\times 114\text{m}
discretized by a
corner-point geometry grid. We consider a heterogeneous anisotropic reservoir
with porosity
0.3
and permeability
{\bm{\underline{K}}}
in a form of a diagonal
matrix with the
x
and
y
components identical and forming three horizontal
layers, and the
z
component forming two horizontal layers, see
Figure
34
. We consider a gas injection in a reservoir
initially unsaturated. A vertical gas injection well perforates a corner of
the reservoir in the
z
direction and a production well is located in
the opposite corner. On the fine scale, the domain is discretized by a grid
of
76\times 48\times 10
elements and on the coarse scale by a grid of

38\times 24\times 5
cells, leading to one refinement level. The process
is simulated to
t_{F}=2000
days with initial time step
\tau^{0}=4.32\times 10^{4}
s, which equals to
0.5
days. Data, constraints, and pressure-volume
properties are adapted from the first SPE comparative solution project model
(SPE1) designed to simulate a three-dimensional black-oil reservoir, given in

[
201
, Tables 1,2, and 3]
. We consider a discretization by the
implicit multi-point finite volume scheme of Section
7.5
with the
blended Newton and fixed-point linearization detailed in Section
7.6
. For the linear
solver of Section
7.7
, we again use the BiCGStab
[
239
]
with an
ILU
\{0\}
preconditioner. Figure
35
shows the evolution of
the gas saturation and of the spatial estimator at
1000
days. Note that, for
the spatial estimator, the data are normalized by max value in order to have
a
[0,1]
range. We observe that the spatial estimator follows the saturation
front though the heterogeneous anisotropic medium with time evolution.

Figure 35:
[Section
7.11.3
] Results on the fine mesh at
1000
days, gas saturation (
left
) and normalized polygonal estimate (
right
)

Adaptive space mesh refinement and stopping criteria for the linear solver

In the standard resolution of our reservoir prototype simulator, the chosen
grid is the fine-scale one and the initial time step, as mentioned before, is
chosen as
\tau^{0}=4.32\times 10^{4}
s. As in the test of Section

7.11.2
, the time step is increased systematically, by
multiplying by
2
, but also controlled by the convergence of the
linearization loop in such a way that we divide it by
2
if a divergence of
the linearization algorithm occurs. We thus stick to this setting and only
focus on the stopping criteria for the linear solver (and not for the linearization
one) and on mesh adaptivity in space (and not in time). For the adaptive
resolution, we start on the coarse-level grid allowing to one refinement
level and we fix
{\delta}_{\mathrm{ref}}=0.7,{\delta}_{\mathrm{deref}}=0.2
in
Algorithm
7.1
.

Figure
36
illustrates the evolution of the approximate gas
saturation at two different time steps. We remark that the refinement follows
the saturation front as times evolves. Additionally, the fact that the light
component G (gas component) can dissolve into the liquid oil phase or
volatilize in the gas phase, we are lead to perform some localized refinement
in zones abandoned by the front of gas saturation.

Figure 36:
[Section
7.11.3
] Gas saturation: results on the adaptive mesh at
500
days (
left
) and
1500
days (
right
)

We show in the left part of Figure
37
, at a fixed time of

500
days and for the first linearization iteration, the evolution of the total
estimator
\left(\eta_{{\rm sp},c}^{n,k,i}+\eta_{{\rm tm},c}^{n,k,i}+\eta_{{\rm lin},c}^{n,k,i}+\eta_{{\rm alg},c}^{n,k,i}+\eta_{{\rm rem},c}^{n,k,i}\right)
with
c={\rm G}

the gas component and the estimators given
in (
7.37
)–(
7.38
), the algebraic
estimator
\eta_{{\rm alg},c}^{n,k,i}
of (
7.37
),
(
7.38f
) (with
j=20
additional steps), and the relative algebraic residual
given by

{\rm{err}}^{n,k,i}_{{\rm alg}}:=\frac{\|\mathbb{A}^{n,k-1}\mathsf{X}^{n,k,i}-\mathsf{B}^{n,k-1}\|}{\|\mathsf{B}^{n,k-1}\|}

with
\mathbb{A}^{n,k-1}\mathsf{X}^{n,k}=\mathsf{B}^{n,k-1}
being
the linear system resulting from the
k
-th iteration of the blended Newton/fixed-point linearization at
time step
t^{n}
, see Section
7.6
. For the standard resolution,
we stop the algebraic iteration using a fixed threshold
{\rm{err}}^{n,k,i}_{{\rm alg}}\leq 10^{-6}
, following our usual practice. In the adaptive
resolution based on Algorithm
7.1
, we fix
\gamma_{\rm alg}=10^{-2}
.

We remark, in the left part of Figure
37
, that the algebraic
estimator steadily decreases, while the total estimator almost stagnates
after about a third of total number of iterations necessary to converge
using the standard stopping criterion. In the right part of
Figure
37
, we depict the cumulated number of BiCGStab
iterations at each time step (the sum of the necessary number of BiCGStab
iterations at each linearization iteration of the time step). We observe a
significant gain with the adaptive stopping criterion.

The necessary linear solver steps and the different CPU times of the adaptive
and standard resolutions are collected in Table
2
. It
shows that the total number of linear solver steps is reduced by
70\%
,
which is an important gain. An important gain is also observed in the CPU
time. We in particular remark that the time spent for the adaptation (AMR and
estimators evaluation) only represents a small part of the actual resolution
time. Globally, a reduction factor of 3.8 of the overall CPU time is obtained
by comparing the adaptive resolution with the standard one. Important
increase of this reduction factor is still to be expected when also the
linearization solver stopping criteria and time step adaptation are used.

Problem (
7.1
)–(
7.12
) is highly involved. For this reason, the existence and uniqueness of the weak solution is supposed in Assumption
7.2
. For simplified problems, existence and uniqueness of the weak solution together with existence, uniqueness, and a priori error estimates for numerical discretizations have been derived in Alt and Luckhaus
[
16
]
, Alt
et al.
